IN MEMORIAM  
 
 
Sarah M. Grant passed away on February 4, 2022. She had lived in Lunenburg since 
2012 and been a member of the Council on Aging from 2013 to 2022.  
Sarah founded one of the Council on Aging’s annual events, the Tea Party, which was a very 
popular event. She was very focused on all the little details that go into a formal tea party. 
Sarah also led craft classes at the Senior Center as she was very talented. She will be missed.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
William J. Gustus passed away in his home on September 19, 2022, surrounded by 
his loving family. Bill owned and operated Settlers Crossing Golf Course in Lunenburg 
and the Westminster Golf & Country Club in Westminster with his wife Laura Caron-
Gustus and business partner Donnie Lyons. Prior to his retirement he was the Town 
Administrator for the Town of Lynnfield, and the Chief Administrative and Financial 
Officer for the Town of Lunenburg.  
 
 
 
 
Longtime dance and physical education teacher, Genevieve C. Nass, 67, died 
Nov. 29, 2022.  Genevieve was born on Sept. 30, 1955, attended Lunenburg 
schools and graduated from Lunenburg High School in 1973.  
Known for the twinkle in her eye and her big smile, she ran her own dance studio 
for more than 40 years and taught physical education for 20 years in the 
Lunenburg Public Schools.  
She looked forward to summers at the Town Beach and also directed the 
Lunenburg Town Beach for many years, acting as a lifeguard, swimming 
instructor and lifeguard trainer.

LUNENBURG PROFILE  
YEAR INCORPORATED-1728 
REGISTERED VOTERS– 9041 
FY’22 TAX RATE- $17.19 
OPEN TOWN GOVERNMENT– 5 MEMBER SELECT BOARD 
ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION– 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY 
ANNUAL TOWN MEETING– 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY 
TOTAL AREA– 26.63 MILES 
Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4 
square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts  approximately  43 miles from  
Boston,  It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the 
Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley and on the north by 
the Town of Townsend. 
 
Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Lake Whalom and the 
former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This 
district was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of 
Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect 
characteristics of these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills Lake area was 
developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. Shirley Reservoir is a public 
water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been modified to year-round 
residences. 
Lunenburg
 
Visit our website at https://www.lunenburgma.gov 
Follow us on social media on Facebook at https://www.facebook.com/lunenburgma

                          ADMINISTRATION 
===PAGE 18===
18 
REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER 
FY22 
It is my pleasure to submit the annual report on the activities of the Office of the Town Manager during 
Fiscal Year 2022. Fiscal Year 2022 started off like most other years however, there were lingering after 
effects of the pandemic. The pandemic brought about a shift in the employment marketplace, causing 
vacancies to last longer and a smaller applicant pool to fill positions. The State extended the allowance of 
virtual public meetings, which most boards and committees continued to use as a matter of convenience and, 
at times, for personal health reasons. The federal stimulus funds that were part of the American Rescue Plan 
Act made their way down to cities and towns and this would result in many discussions in how best to use 
these windfall monies. Driver shortages, supply chain issues, and increased demand for items disrupted our 
Pay As You Throw program, severely impacted the delivery schedule of vehicles/equipment the Department 
of Public Works, Fire Department, and Police Department ordered and spiked costs for construction projects.  
Despite the ripple effects of the pandemic, the Town continued to provide a high level of services and was 
another year of progress. Below I would like to highlight the topics that were addressed and the 
accomplishments within the Office of the Town Manager: 
 In July of 2021, as part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed and voted to accept the 
Town Manager’s annual goals for FY22. The Town Manager’s goals for FY22 included continuing to 
take the necessary steps towards the redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, working with 
the Select Board on creating a means tested senior citizen tax relief program, and action items related to 
enhancing facilities maintenance and management.  A majority of the action items related to these three 
goals were completed.  In regards to 925 Massachusetts Avenue, the Town Manager continued to work 
Joe McLaughlin of BETA Group, MassDEP, and MassDevelopment about possible cleanup funding. In 
the end we worked with the EPA and this project was put on the list of projects in line for federal funding 
through Targeted Brownfields Assessment funding. The funding would pay for removal of the 
underground tanks, limited soil removal and make the Town eligible for future cleanup funds. This work 
is slated to be completed in FY 2023. In addition, a survey was completed for the property and after 
much due diligence to eliminate any possibility that the Town would be legally responsible for cleanup, 
the Town foreclosed on the property, which was a necessary step to receive the federal funding from the 
EPA.As it related to one of the action items under the Town Manager’s annual goals for Facilities, the 
Town Manager followed the Town Charter process for an Administrative Organization Plan and after a 
public hearing process, received approval at the May 2022 Annual Town Meeting to separate Facilities 
from the Department of Public Works. The third goal, to assist the Select Board with a means tested 
senior citizen tax relief program was initiated and the Principal Assessor presented information from 
other communities with similar programs to the working group. Ultimately additional time was needed to 
develop the framework of the program and its effects on taxpayers, so this goal was carried forward to 
FY 2023. 
 In July 2021, the Select Board set their goals for the upcoming year: they amended their FY21 goal/work 
on the old Primary School at 30 School Street with additional action items such as engaging with a 
landscape architecture firm to discuss a vision of multi-use, passive recreation/ green space project at the 
site and all necessary steps to bring the associated costs to the 2022 Annual Town Meeting. Another goal 
was to work with the Town Manager and Finance Director to establish a means-tested senior citizen 
property tax exemption or abatement. The last goal was to develop a policy designed to encourage 
volunteerism in Town, specifically for town boards, committees, and commissions. A Request for 
Proposals was put out twice for selection of a landscape architect for 30 School Street and ultimately 
architect Ray Dunetz was chosen for the project. Various staff and community meetings led to a 
conceptual design that requested additional funding at the May 2022 Annual Town Meeting, but funding 
was only approved for abatement and demolition of the structure and the associated engineering costs. 
The goal of developing a policy designed to encourage volunteerism was discussed at a workshop but the   
Board was split on the language of the policy so no action was taken during fiscal year 2022 on this goal. 
 At the end of Fiscal Year 2021 the Town received a letter from Mass Housing  that Pond View 
Commons, LLC had filed an application for a 40B housing project. The proposed development would 
consist of 200 units of rental housing on 18.5 acres of land located at 318, 390 & 400 Howard Street.   
===PAGE 19===
19 
 August 2022 began with a workshop on Chapter 40B led by Town Counsel Adam Costa on the role of the 
Zoning Board of Appeals would provide in the application process. The Town was required to submit a 
response to the letter from Mass Housing by September 2, 2021, which an 88-page response was 
submitted that included numerous letters from residents, town boards, town counsel, and the Town 
Manager. Mass Housing reviewed the town’s response, requested information from the applicant and 
ultimately granted a positive 40B project eligibility in March of 2022. From that point on, the applicant 
filed a comprehensive permit application with the Zoning Board of Appeals. The application is still 
ongoing as of writing this report.  
 The Town Manager submitted recommendations to the Select Board on adoption or amendment of 
various policies throughout the year, including the Capital Budget Policy changing the threshold for 
individual projects from $10,000 to $20,000, a new Senior Citizen Property Work Off Program Policy 
which would change to an internal function of reviewing the applications by the Assistant Town 
Manager versus a committee, amendments to the Liquor License Regulations, the Boot Drive Policy, 
MA Emergency Paid Sick Leave, and Sign Policy. In April the Board finalized a new Commemorative 
Policy and began working on their third goal of a Volunteerism Policy. In May a new Tailings Policy, 
which addresses uncashed checks issued by the Town, was adopted and in June a new Ambulance 
Billing Collection Policy was adopted. 
 As previously mentioned, Fiscal Year 2022 was a difficult year for retaining staff and filling vacancies. 
The pandemic created opportunities for employees to move to new careers and made it a more competitive 
marketplace for employers. The Assistant Town Manager/HR Director who is responsible for advertising 
vacancies and working with the relevant departments to bring on new employees was constantly reviewing 
applications, interviewing and trying to fill vacancies throughout the year. In August 2021 the Veterans 
Agent, IT Director (shared between Town/School), and a Board Clerk resigned. The Veterans Agent 
position was filled in September, the Town Manager hired an outside consultant in September to fill the 
role of the IT Director, and additional Board Clerks were hired throughout the year. In September 
Principal Assessor Sue Byrne announced her retirement effective end of September and Christine Kumar 
was hired in September to fill this position, but resigned at the end of November. The position was filled 
with a temporary Assessor, Rena Swezey, from December 2021 until March of 2022 when Christopher 
Carroll was selected to fill the permanent position. In October the Principal Clerk for the Town 
Accountant retired and this position changed to an Assistant Town Accountant position after the 
November Special Town Meeting and was filled in January 2022. Also in October the Recreation Director 
Kayla Wright resigned and Rob Thompson was hired in December to fill this position. In March DPW 
Director Rob Oliva resigned and this position was unfilled until May of 2022 when Bill Bernard was 
appointed. Also in May of 2022 Conservation Administrator Matt Marro retired and Nadia Madden was 
hired in May to fill this position. The Police Department also saw staffing changes with three Reserve and 
Full-Time Officers leaving for other positions, and these were filled at different times during the year. The 
DPW had up to three vacant Heavy Equipment Operator positions at all times during the year. The 
Council on Aging and Public Access also had vacancies for Van Drivers and Videographers during the 
fiscal year as well. The positions that were new to the FY22 budget, including the Assistant to the Sewer 
Business Manager position, Board/Committee Clerk positions, Remote Meeting Coordinator, Trash & 
Recycling Coordinator, an additional Police Officer, an additional Firefighter/Paramedic, and restoration 
of a Facilities Technician position, were all filled at various times during the year. 
 The Town Manager, the Assistant Town Manager, and the relevant staff worked together on putting a 
variety of projects out to bid in FY 2022, including the Landscape Architect Services for 30 School 
Street (put out to bid twice due to no response on the first bid), Master Planning Services (no response to 
bid), Master Planning/Landscape Architectural Services for Marshall Park, the Public Safety Building 
Chiller project, Town Building Cleaning Services, Town Beach Building Abatement work, Town Beach 
Building Alterations (bids came in over budget), DPW Road Materials, Library LED Lighting, 30 School 
Street Building Abatement work, and LED Streetlight Design and Project Management Services (all bids 
rejected). 
 This year was another productive year for infrastructure projects in Lunenburg. Through a $260,000 
grant the Town Manager received through the Complete Streets grant program, sidewalk work on 
Memorial Drive, the intersection of Main and Oak Avenue, and the sidewalks connecting Town Hall and  
===PAGE 20===
20 
and the sidewalks connecting Town Hall and then over upper common in front of 1 Main Street was              
completed. The front decking at the entrance of Town Hall was also replaced. Electrical work was done in the 
Town Hall basement to address bringing certain wiring up to code and to eliminate unnecessary old wiring. 
The environmental consultant hired for the 104 Fairview Road project (also known as the Woodruff Property) 
filed a Notice of Intent with the Conservation Commission and the Town was issued an Order of Conditions 
in order to proceed with putting this work out to bid. The Pleasant Street Bridge project, which was funded 
through a Small Bridge Grant, was completed by the fall of 2021 and the MassDOT bridge project on Mass. 
Avenue was completed later that fiscal year. Through a $50,000 state earmark, hazardous abatement work 
was undertaken at the old Primary School at 30 School Street. Hazardous material abatement was also 
completed at the Town Beach building. The Town also began a LED lighting project at the Library through a 
$40,000 state earmark received in FY22. Due to inflated construction costs that came about during the 
pandemic, the Flat Hill Road Culvert project estimated cost increased and the Town applied for a $500,000 
Small Bridge Grant to supplement the local appropriation. 
 Related to procurement of services, the Town entered into a contract with a new trash & recycling hauler, 
E.L. Harvey, on July 1, 2021. The Town was contracted with Casella for the previous ten years and the 
first few months of the new contract with E.L Harvey required significant attention to address prior 
practices, such as pick-up on private roads that were not on the routes reviewed with Casella, to iron out. 
Due to the increase in cost of the solid waste removal under the new contract, bag prices were required to 
increase by 15% and this went into effect mid-August. In addition to the transition to a new vendor, the 
pandemic’s effect on the supply of replacement trucks and driver shortages also impacted service 
delivery.  
 The Town Manager’s Office applied for and received the MIIA Risk Management Grant for $10,000 for 
Microsoft Office 365 licenses and $6,600 for a Recycling Dividends Grant that helped fund the Trash & 
Recycling Coordinator position. 
 The Town Manager and staff worked closely with various industrial and commercial projects, including a 
372,000 square foot warehouse/distribution facility called Lunenburg Central located on 475 Leominster -
Shirley Road and an adult use retail cannabis license applicant called Uma Flowers at 43 Summer Street. 
The Select Board voted on the Host Community Agreement with Uma Flowers in December 2021, which 
is a required step to get licensed by the Cannabis Control Commission. 
  Throughout the year the Select Board addressed various town property related items, including 
 The Select Board authorized Town Counsel to send two letters of Notice of Trespass to property owners 
who were encroaching on town properties; the first was for boulders placed along the edge of and into the 
public right of way at the rear of 993 Massachusetts Avenue and the other was for a shed at 38 Pierce 
Avenue erected on town property. As a result of no action from the property owner at 993 Massachusetts 
Avenue and on advice of Town counsel, the Select Board approved taking legal action to address the 
encroachment of boulders within the public right of way. 
 The Select Board approved a survey requested by the Conservation Commission regarding the boundary 
between the Cowdrey Conservation property and 192 Townsend Harbor Road to rectify a possible 
encroachment and the identification of other possible encroachments onto the Cowdrey property from 
another property on Townsend Harbor Road and Woodland Drive. These possible encroachments were 
discovered while the commission was performing site walks. 
 Authorized changes were made to some Assessors ’ records of town properties that were incorrectly 
described as under the care of the Conservation Commission. The parcels were 510 Townsend Harbor 
Road (split into two separate parcels)

 Over the course of the fiscal year the Town received the full allotment of ARPA (American Rescue 
Plan Act) funding through the Coronavirus State and Local Fiscal Recovery Funds program in the

potential ARPA projects and presented these to the Select Board. The Town Manager worked with a 
company called Capital Strategic Solutions and they presented an outline of American Rescue Plan Act 
funding categories and their services to the Board in February. The Board would continue to have 
many discussions about how to spend the funding. These discussions are still occurring in FY 2023.

ultimately decided to reject all bids. The Town Manager prepared and submitted the Green

and funds towards the purchase of a hybrid police cruiser with a grand total request of $187,140 The

 In October the Select Board met with the TC Passios Building Design Committee to review their three 
draft warrant articles for the November Special Town Meeting, including an article to hear an interim 
report on the committee’s progress, an article to authorize borrowing $28.9M for renovation of the 
building for town offices, school uses, and community uses, and an article to fund the design and 
engineering required to construct a new building for the consolidation of town offices, school uses, and 
community space at the location of the building and land known as the TC Passios, amount to be 
determined by Town Meeting. Ultimately the Committee presented an interim report but both funding 
articles were passed over at the Special Town Meeting. After the Town Meeting, the Committee came 
back to the Select Board and asked for a revised name to the Lunenburg Municipal Building Design 
Committee and revised charge to include multiple buildings rather than just the TC Passios Building.  
 Prior to the November Special Town Meeting Select Board member Jim Marino submitted his resignation 
due to his moving out of town. The Town Manager and the Board thanked him for all his years of service 
and wished him well. The Board decided not to hold a special election to fill the vacancy.  
 The Special Town Meeting in November 2021 included a number of articles such as amending the 
FY22 budget, payment of prior year bills, funding for the first year of the collective bargaining 
agreement with the Firefighters Union, amending the Sewer Enterprise budget, transfer available funds 
to the Sewer Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund, the three TCP Building Design warrant articles 
mentioned above, acquiring temporary and permanent utility easements for construction of the culvert 
on Flat Hill Road, approval for Select Board to acquire an easement related to the Lunenburg Central 
parcel on Leominster Shirley Road, amendment to the sewer service area map by adding Marshall Park, 
transferring 970 Rear New West Townsend Road to the care and custody of the Conservation 
Commission, amending the Salary Administration Plan Bylaw to add certain positions, and an article 
from the Historical Commission to amend to the Code of Lunenburg, Chapter 134, entitled Demolition 
Delay, subsections 4-D and 5, and 4 articles to amend the Protective Bylaws.

 In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday 
entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.  Throughout the year, the 
Board reviews Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers, flammable storage licenses, pole 
petitions, tollbooth requests, First Right of Refusal Requests for Chapter 61 properties, and Dangerous 
Dog hearings. 
 In January the Town Manager presented a Five-Year Forecast and Capital Plan for the funding of 20 
projects totaling $1,740,049 and a 10 year look ahead at projected capital needs. In early March the 
Town Manager revised the recommendation to include 3 more projects for a grand total of $2,162,849.  
 In February, the Select Board met with State Representative Kushmerek, State Senator John 
Cronin, and State Representative Sena regarding FY 2023 budget priorities and any updates on 
State budget or House/Senate Bills. The Town Manager presented her recommendations to the 
Board on projects to submit as budget priorities and the Board voted a final list to submit to the 
delegation that included the following projects:  $330,000 for Flat Hill Road culvert; $371,000 for 
hazardous materials abatement and demolition of the old Primary School building; $100,000 for 
Senior Center building improvements; $232,800 for the Air Conditioning for the Primary School. 
The legislature included the $100,000 for the Senior Center building improvements for an ADA 
outdoor patio and the Town received $100,000 in a bond bill towards the Primary School 
building.  
 The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2023 to the Finance Committee and 
Selectmen in early February.  Part of the Town Manager’s recommendation was to continue to fund the 
Other Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the 
amount of $266,696, an additional $50,000 towards Pavement Management, fund the regular 
Stabilization fund in the amount of $100,000, and $450,000 towards the Special Purpose Stabilization 
Fund. The FY2023 budget also included the hiring of an additional Full Time Patrolman, an additional 
Full Time Firefighter, a new Facilities Director position, 2 part time Library Assistants for the Library to 
open on Fridays, a new part time Assistant Meal Site Manager position, a part time Police 
Administrative Assistant position, a part time IT Administrative Assistant position, a part time 
Conservation Administrative Assistant, a $40K decrease for Solid Waste/Recycling Program, and a 
3.11% increase for the Lunenburg Public Schools and Monty Tech. The total omnibus budget for FY23 
that was approved in May was $41,568,744.   
 In May, following the annual election, the Board reorganized and vote Michael-Ray Jeffreys as the 
new Chair, Tom Alonzo as Vice Chair and Lou Franco as Clerk. Heather Sroka and Todd Dwyer were 
elected as the additional two members.   
 Powers and Sullivan presented the results of the FY 2021 Annual Audit and presented that the Town 
had addressed any prior year comments. 
 The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY 2023.   
 The Town Manager’s Office submitted three Certificate to Alter applications for town buildings 
within the Architectural Preservation District in FY 2022: the Town Hall for a building envelope 
project, the Ritter Building for an ADA Ramp and replacement of windows, and to demolish the 
vacant building known as the old Primary School on 30 School Street. The Select Board expressed 
frustration at the inaction of the Architectural Preservation District Commission’s failure to act in a 
timely manner regarding the Certificate to Alter application for the Ritter Building project submitted 
in December 2021 as well as the APDC’s comments on social media regarding the 30 School Street 
demolition project. Ultimately the APDC approved the Certificate to Alter application to demolish the 
old Primary School on March 21, 2022, denied the Ritter Building Certificate to Alter application on 
June 27, 2022 and approved the Town Hall Certificate to Alter application on July 6, 2022. The Town 
is still pursuing an alteration to the Ritter Building including replacement of all the windows with  
historic looking windows to become more energy efficient and install an ADA compliant ramp to the 
entrance of the building, but this has been delayed due to the retirement of the Town’s original 
architect. This continues to be a priority in 2023.

Six and a half years has passed since I was appointed your Town Manager and I am thankful for the many 
residents I have met, work with on various committees, and represent on a daily basis overseeing the 
operations of the Town. Similar to last year, the pandemic and neighborly response that is demonstrated by the 
residents of Lunenburg, is inspiring. Thank you to all of our community members who continue to help their 
fellow residents throughout this pandemic. 
I have the privilege of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents and 
business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public.  The 
accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without the 
cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government turn.  
Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson and Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for all 
your hard work and keeping the office running smoothly.  I would also like to thank the committee and board 
members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town. 
Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the current and former department heads that led our various 
departments in Fiscal Year 2022:  Thomas Gammel, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Robert Oliva, 
former DPW Director; Bill Bernard, current DPW Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance 
Director; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Sue Byrne, former Principal Assessor; CJ Carroll, Principal 
Assessor; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA Director; Muir Haman, Library Director; 
Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk;  and all their staff for their talent and working together to make our town run as 
efficiently as possible.  Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses that make Lunenburg a wonderful 
place to visit, work, and live. 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
Heather R. Lemieux

Town Manager

BOARD OF ASSESSORS 
The Board of Assessors is responsible for establishing the value of property, both real and personal, for 
taxation purposes for the town. Assessed values are subject to a five-year revaluation and interim year 
adjustments for certification by the Department of Revenue.  The Assessors grant all statutory exemptions for 
Senior and Surviving Spouse, Blind and Veterans. They act on all abatement applications to property values.  
The Assessors are responsible for defending all appeals to the Appellate Tax Board. They also administer all 
special assessments qualifying farm and forest management programs. Assessments in Massachusetts are 
based on full and fair cash value as of January 1st. 
The Assessors analyze all sales from 2021 for FY 23 valuations.  Market value has seen increases in the sales 
of homes in town.  Properties have been selling over the assessed valuations reflecting an overall increase to 
market value of the town. The next town wide recertification will occur this year for FY 2024.  
Each property in town has a property record card on file with all the components that go into the valuation of 
the parcel - such as land size, building description, sales information, etc.  We recommend that the taxpayers 
review their property field card annually to ensure all the information is up to date and correct. The property 
record cards are available in our office or online through the GIS maps. 
The Department of Revenue certified the results of assessment data to be within their statistical requirements 
and the tax bills went out on time.  The tax rate went from $17.19 to $14.62 for FY 2023. 
The office continues the process of mandated cyclical inspections of all properties throughout the town.  This 
is achieved by inspections, visiting the properties, and by means of sales verification, building permits, 
occupancy permits and abatement inspections.  The Board of Assessors strives to maintain accuracy in the 
assessment to sales ratio as well as assessment uniformity. 
The office staff strives to continue to provide good service that the Lunenburg taxpayers have come to expect. 
Respectfully submitted, 
Richard Letarte, Chairman      Christine C.Higdon, Member      Kevin Fish, Member

BUILDING DEPARTMENT 
The Building Department is located in the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960 Massachusetts Avenue. 
The department consists of Casey Burlingame who is the Building Commissioner and Zoning Enforcement 
Officer. Lisa Normandin is the Administrative Assistant to the Building Commissioner. Jack Biery is the 
Electrical Inspector, and Gary Williams is the Plumbing and Gas Inspector.  
The Building Department strives to ensure all work done in the Town of Lunenburg is performed to state 
mandated regulations and codes, as well as local zoning bylaws. In addition to Building, Plumbing, Gas, 
and Electrical permits, the Building Department also oversees annual inspections for places of assembly, 
safety inspections for licensed on-premise alcohol establishments and Forms of Intent required for business 
certificates issued from the Town Clerk office.  
In fiscal year 2022 the following permits were issued: 
620 Building Permits  
282 Electrical Permits 
107 Plumbing Permits 
127 Gas Permits 
1,136 total permits issued 
The state of Massachusetts is currently utilizing the 2015 International Building Code and 2015 
International Residential Code, as amended by Massachusetts. The Town continues to utilize ViewPoint 
online permitting software and all applications for work requiring a permit can be done online. In addition, 
paper applications are available at the Building Department office. 
CEMETERY COMMISSION 
The Cemetery Commission and Superintendent Todd Holman met regularly throughout the year to discuss 
cemetery business.  Projects that were worked on included surveying the Goodrich family burial yard on the 
corner of Fish and Lincoln Streets.  The survey established the property boundaries in preparation of 
extending the existing stone wall to enclose the burial plots.  The final resting places of seven additional 
family members were located using ground penetrating radar.  An appropriate grave marker will be placed 
identifying all the interred in due time.  A ground penetrating radar survey was performed at the Arnold/
Litch family burial yard on West Street Terrace, and the seven burial plots were located.   
The project to prune and restore the historic hedges that line Holman Street at the North Cemetery began 
this year with the expectation it will be fully completed by the end of next year.  Monuments honoring the 
Revolutionary War soldiers that are buried at the North and South Cemeteries were erected.   
The Commissioners would like to thank the following people and businesses whose help was invaluable 
this year: DPW Director William Bernard for his guidance and support during the year.  Superintendent 
Todd Holman for his continued dedication to the care of the town cemeteries and to the service he provides 
for families in their time of need. Seasonal Laborers Alexis Dimond and Danielle Peterson for the 
dedication and hard work they put in this year in helping the Superintendent maintain the cemeteries. 
Robert Ullo for his assistance in plowing snow away from the entrance to South Cemetery. Dave Passios 
for his volunteer efforts in keeping the well-pumps in good working order. Leominster Monument Co. for 
their continued assistance whenever it is needed. Mrs. Helen Balcom for her services in planting, 
maintaining, and watering the flowers that decorate the entrances to both cemeteries. 
The Cemetery Commission maintains an online mapping system for both cemeteries that can be found at: 
www.burialsearch.com 
William Tyler, Chairman  
Nancy- Foss Yasko, Vice- Chairman 
David Dorren, Clerk

CONSERV ATION COMMISSION 
The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven member commission, appointed to  three year terms by 
the Select Board, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL c. 40, § 8C. The 
Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL c. 131, § 40 and Chapter 239 of the local bylaws 
for the protection of wetlands.  The Commission also manages and controls town-owned lands retained for conservation 
and passive recreation use. 
The year 2022 was as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and meetings for a 
wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep projects and larger 
scale industrial/commercial proposals. 
The Commission held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to meetings to 
familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The Commission continues to 
work with the Town on familiarizing itself with the continually changing storm water mandates from the EPA and 
working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification as well as aiding with the 
community implementations the “MS4” regulations. 
The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans developed using grants as outlined in 
the last two annual reports. The upcoming management efforts will be concentrated on the Rayno property on 
Northfield Road and the Hawes property on Hollis Road. The stewardship plan for the Hollis Road property is oriented 
toward enhancing bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the DCR "Forestry for 
the Birds" program.  The forest stewardship plans which provide environmental management including timber-stand 
management were developed by Gary Gouldrup of New England Forestry Consultants. The Commission continues to 
hold public forums on the implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management. The funding from 
the sale of harvested timber provides the seed money to make it possible for the Commission to apply for grants to 
maintain and improve Lunenburg’s conservation lands.  
The Commission continues to implement a Recreational Trails Program grant providing color-coded trail markers and 
coordinated “You Are Here” trail map signs to expand the safe use of Lunenburg’s existing trail network.  Regional 
trail system map signs show how trails are interconnected among multiple properties. The project also provides 
informational kiosks and emergency access gates.  During 2022 trail maps were developed for and continue to be 
refined in detail for eleven conservation properties.  These trail maps are posted on the new Conservation Commission 
Trails website, on informational kiosks on the properties, and on “You Are Here” trail map signs at trail heads and trail 
intersections. The Commission would like to express our gratitude for all the hard-working volunteers that work so 
enthusiastically in maintaining and improving Lunenburg’s conservation lands. The Lunenburg Snow Riders continue 
to support the maintenance and improvement of the trail system on Lunenburg’s conservation lands.  The club replaced 
a bridge on the Cowdrey Conservation Area.  The club was awarded a Recreational Trails Program grant that will erect 
five new trail head kiosks, improve the cart paths on the Lane Conservation Area, level and de-stump Small Town 
Forest trails and gravel the parking lots at the Cowdrey Conservation Area.  The Conservation Commission will fund 
the material for this work using Timber Rights Funds.  
The Commission has continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills on the implementations of their 
lake management plans.  These plans set goals and action items designed to give a better picture of the overall health of 
the lakes and define various strategies to ensure continued protection of these valuable resources. 
During the year of 2022, change of administration occurred along with an additional staff member. In the month of May 
Conservation Administrator Matt Marro retired after many years of service to the Conservation Commission and Town 
of Lunenburg. After his departure, Nadia Madden had taken his place as Conservation Administrator. The town 
additionally hired a Conservation Administrative Assistant, Rebecca Manley, who has been a great help for the 
Conservation Administrator. In November 2022, Anna Petrie was hired as Conservation Administrator after Nadia’s 
departure. 
The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!  
Respectfully Submitted, 
Bob Pease, Chair                                      Mary Wilson, Vice-Chair                  Richard Tremblay, Clerk 
Richard Bursch                                         Catie Childs                                       Michael Larouche 
                                                                  Jeffrey Viviano 
                                              Anna Petrie, Conservation Administrator

COUNCIL ON AGING 
FY22 
July 1, 2021 – June 30, 2022 
The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved 
quality of life for all seniors through supportive services, programs, and 
education.  Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts 
General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the 
administration of elder programs or services and to develop, coordinate, 
and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues. 
The FY22 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, 
Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative Assistant and 
Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal 
Site Coordinator; Elsa Watson and Ann Penney, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, David Gallagher, Peter 
Tomlinson, and Kimberly Moore, MART Van Drivers.   
The Council on Aging Board is an eleven-member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Select Board. The 
FY22 Council on Aging Board members were Chairperson Deb Lincoln,  Vice-Chairperson Jane Rabbitt, Barbara 
Brown, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Pete Lincoln, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, John Pesenti, Susan O’Sullivan, and 
Judith Tarbell.  Jacquelyn Dwyer joined the board in May of 2022 to fill the remaining term of Sarah Grant. 
The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House Supporters 
Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and Montachusett 
Regional Transit Authority.  
We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Council on Aging.  In FY22 we had 40 volunteers who 
contributed 1183.25 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on Wheels program, 
program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, Discovery Center navigators, and friendly visitors/
phone calls.  We greatly appreciate their service. 
 
In FY22 we offered a variety of programs including Meals on Wheels, congregate lunch and breakfast programs, 
transportation, health and wellness, arts and crafts, intergenerational, bocce, shuffleboard, cards and games, cultural, 
information and referral, Memory Café, health insurance counseling, and social events both at the senior center and 
offsite to people age 50+.  There were approximately 1337 people who utilized the senior center in FY22.  Those 1337 
people compiled 19,162 units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.   
The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at 
www.mycommunityonline.com , or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town Hall, 
Library, Post Office, local churches, and various businesses throughout the community. 
We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of 
supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders. 
Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc.  This group provides funding for new 
living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, and garden upkeep at the Eagle House 
Senior Community Center.  Also special thanks to all our outside agencies, especially the Lunenburg Police and Fire 
Departments, for their time and funding for support of programming for our clients. 
I want to thank the staff and volunteers at the Eagle House Senior Community Center for their caring and commitment to 
our Lunenburg elders.  There are 350 Councils on Aging in the Commonwealth, and I consider our Lunenburg staff and 
volunteers amongst the “very best of the best” in Massachusetts. 
Respectfully Submitted 
Susan Doherty, Director 
Lunenburg Council on Aging

CULTURAL COUNCIL 
 
The Lunenburg Cultural Council with grants from Massachusetts Cultural Council has fully funded or 
contributed to twenty-three art, music, cultural and science programs to benefit the residents of Lunenburg and 
surrounding communities.  
These include events such as children’s programs at the Public Library, adult programs at the Eagle House, the 
85th Regional Art Show at Fitchburg Art Museum, an art therapy program for cancer patients and survivors, 
tuition for one student for the 2022 Cannon Theater Children’s Program, and the Discovery Museum, among 
other programs.  
FY22 grants totaled $10,640. Because of the pandemic, some FY21 programs were moved into FY22 and 
those were also funded ($1180), with only a few cancelled due to the pandemic. 
We are looking forward to the events in FY23. Grants were approved for twenty-two programs totaling 
$14,166.  
In addition, the Cultural Council will be sponsoring several programs of our own, including two day-long art 
classes which will be held at the Public Library this spring.  
We are looking forward to holding more events during the year and hope all will enjoy and benefit from the 
cultural events in Lunenburg.

DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
 
The Department of Public Works is the resources of the Highway, Cemetery, Sewer, Vehicle Maintenance, Facilities, 
and Parks divisions combined to maintain roads, bridges, sewer infrastructure, Town buildings, and parks and to provide 
services essential to the welfare and acceptable quality of life for the citizens of Lunenburg. 
Fiscal Year 2022 was transitional year for the DPW with the Director, Robert Oliva, two Heavy Equipment Operators, 
and the Recreation Director leaving for other employment opportunities and the hiring of a new DPW Director, William 
Bernard.  Major changes continued in early Fiscal Year 2023 with the establishment of a new Facilities Department and 
the removal of the Facilities and Parks Divisions from under the umbrella of the DPW.  Despite the upheaval, the DPW 
accomplished much work while providing excellent services.  One mainstay throughout all was the DPW Executive 
Assistant, Samantha Lenox, who provided business and professional support to all staff. 
Departmental Mission: 
The mission of the Department of Public Works is to provide high quality, cost effective, efficient, and safe services to 
protect, preserve and improve the Town’s roadway infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and 
enhances the current and future social and economic needs of the community; to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality 
environment for the Town’s citizens and visitors; and to provide environmentally sound means of solid waste and 
wastewater disposal.    
Departmental Goals and Objectives 
 Be prepared to respond to any weather, roadway infrastructure, or accident-related emergency that may arise on a 
twenty-four hour per day, three hundred and sixty-five day per year basis. 
 Maintain and enhance a highly qualified and dedicated staff of public works professionals. 
 Provide preventative maintenance in a proactive manner to reduce reactive maintenance as follows: 
 Maintain and update the sewer system and associated pumping stations. 
 Maintain and repair roadways in accordance with the new (2022) pavement management plan. 
 Maintain and repair sidewalks in accordance with the 2020 PROW ADA Transition plan. 
 Increase the number of roadsides mowed by developing a roadside maintenance plan. 
 Increase the number of catch basin and stormwater structures cleaned and the number of miles of roadways swept 
per the June 2020 Operations and Maintenance Plan prepared by Comprehensive Environmental, Inc. 
 Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and plans for 
rehabilitation. 
 Increase training offered by our insurance provider to obtain credits to lessen premiums.   
 Identify new sources (Federal, State, and other grants) of funding to supplement the operational budget. 
 Continue to maintain a high level of courteous communication and productive interaction between members of 
boards and commissions as well as Town residents. 
 Strive to modernize the operations of the Department by obtaining and using the most up to date equipment and 
methods, including asset management. 
Highway Division 
The highway division provided daily maintenance and repairs of roads, drainage, signs, trees, roadsides, sewers, and 
more.  They assisted all other Departments as requested and responded to emergencies 24 hours per day as needed.  The 
Highway Superintendent, Joseph Progin, provided outstanding leadership, knowledge of the town’s infrastructure and 
demonstrated true concern for the citizens of Lunenburg.  The DPW Director reviewed and approved applications for 
permits for Road Openings, Street Improvements, Trench Safety, Sewer Connections, and Sewer Extensions. He 
reviewed applications for driveway installations, site plans, stormwater BMP's, building demolition, and sign 
installations for DPW related issues. 
Bridge L-17-013 (6T2) - Pleasant Street Over Pearl Hill Brook 
The replacement of the bridge was completed in the fall of 2021.  Funding for the reconstruction of the Pleasant Street 
Bridge was obtained through the MassDOT Small Bridge Program.  The bridge was designed by our engineering 
consultant, VHB, and R. Bates & Son was the construction contractor with construction beginning in May 2021. 
===PAGE 60===
60 
 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
Pavement Management  
The Town first developed a Pavement Management Plan in 2009.  This plan was updated in 2015 and defined a yearly 
paving plan through Fiscal Year 2023 and included a $4,000,000 voter authorized debt exclusion.  The goal of the plan 
was to achieve an overall town-wide Road Surface Rating of 72-75 out of 100.  In July of 2022 the DPW contracted with 
BETA Group, Inc. to have them provide a new pavement management plan as the current plan was ending and the debt 
exclusion funding had been exhausted.  Although the work was completed in Fiscal Year 2023 and not Fiscal Year 2022, 
it is worth revealing that BETA Group surveyed our roads using LIDAR and reported an overall Road Surface Rating of 
84.75.  The Project Manager for BETA Group commented “it is one of the highest scores I’ve seen in New England over 
the past 12 years that I’ve been doing this”.  The DPW is proud to have successfully administered the plan and 
anticipates carrying out the newly developed plan in a similar manner. 
The following roads were paved with hot mix asphalt this year: 
Brown Ave.                      Goodrich Street                             Highland Street                              Main Street  
Memorial Drive                Oak Avenue                                  Pleasant Street                    
Storm Water 
Staying in compliance with our EPA NPDES MS4 Permit continues to remain an important function of the DPW.  Along 
with the Storm Water Task Force, Land Use Director, and our Storm Water Consultant, the DPW works to meet the 
obligations of the permit and required reporting each year.  The DPW provides maintenance and care of the Town’s 
storm water management systems.  This includes, but is not limited to catch basin cleaning, street sweeping, pipe jetting, 
replacing pipes, and swale cleaning.  The Department responds to calls for clogged catch basins and culverts to help 
control ponding and flooding. 
Tree Removal   
A Tree Risk Management Inventory of almost 1,200 trees on the roads in Lunenburg was completed in 2015.  The 
inventory prioritizes the removal and/or pruning of the trees.  However, storm damage and natural attrition creates a 
backlog of tree work that exceeds the Department’s allotted budget for tree removal.  The Department strives to fulfill 
each resident’s request for tree work but must prioritize work based upon the determined degree of danger to person and 
property and location.  The DPW will continue to act in a reactionary manner as needs arise.  All tree work other than 
brush removal, chipping, and the removal of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the lack of a 
bucket truck or crane and a licensed tree professional.  Each spring and after storms, the DPW staff remove and dispose 
of woody debris within the Town’s right-of-way.  An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the 
burden of the Town by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.  The 
DPW appreciates and thanks Unitil for their responses to remove hazardous trees when asked to do so. 
Roadside Vegetation Maintenance 
The DPW staff responded to many requests to cut and clear overgrown vegetation from roadsides.  This work is done 
using lawn mowers, saws, and other hand tools as the existing roadside mower was disabled.   A new utility tractor with 
a boom rotary mower attachment (22-foot horizontal reach and 19.5-foot vertical reach) was ordered in September of 
2022.   The DPW is developing a town-wide program of selective curbside mowing and vegetation clearing using the 
new tractor and boom mower that will significantly enhance our roadside vegetation maintenance program.   
Landfill 
The closed and capped landfill off Youngs Road is inspected by a consultant semiannually and remains in compliance 
with the requirements of the Massachusetts Division of Environmental Protection (DEP).  Gas monitoring and 
groundwater contamination sampling and testing is conducted by a consultant.  The landfill site including the perimeter 
fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel. 
Adjacent to the closed landfill is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be 
hazardous waste by the EPA.  The DEP has issued a Beneficial Use Determination (BUD) permit which allows the 
storage of these materials.  A consultant performs the yearly testing of these materials.   
On seven Saturdays in the fall and the spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage Yard Waste Days.  Residents may 
drop off brush, leaves, and other organic materials for composting.    
 
 
===PAGE 61===
61 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
Solid Waste 
Solid waste (trash) is collected curbside weekly under the Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program by our vendor E.L. 
Harvey & Sons, Inc.  Recyclable materials are also collected curbside weekly by Harvey.  Pay as You Throw bags may 
be purchased at Hannaford Supermarket, Smokestack Roasters, Maki Building Center, Honey Farms, and Jaxx Country 
Variety.  Residents may call E. L. Harvey to arrange the pickup of bulky items for a fee.  The DPW remains the point of 
contact between residents and E. L. Harvey.  Residents may dispose of household hazardous wastes for a fee at the 
Devens Regional Household Hazardous Waste Collection Center on the published dates and times. 
Lake Shirley Dam 
In the early 1990’s, with the structure in need of repair, The Lake Shirley Management Corporation Association turned 
over ownership of the dam to the Town.  After the completion of the total reconstruction of the dam, the responsibility 
for the maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping were completed by a small number of volunteers from the Lake 
Association.  Compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well as an 
emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of the Department of Public Works in 2004-2005.   The dam is 
inspected by a professional engineer licensed in Massachusetts.  The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report, 
and we remain in compliance with regulations. 
Winter Operations 
The DPW was prepared for and responded to all snow and ice weather events with the goal of providing a high level of 
service in a timely manner while at the same time trying to minimize costs.  Lunenburg joins with 17 other towns in a 
cooperative bid to obtain the best possible pricing for rock salt.  However, the price of rock salt continues to steadily 
increase every year.  The DPW maintains a small fleet of snow removal equipment (trucks, plows, spreaders, loader, 
backhoe, and sidewalk plow).  The fleet is supplemented with vendors as needed.  Due to breakdowns of the aged 
Town’s fleet of large dump trucks, more vendors were needed; thus, adding to costs. 
The clearing of snow and ice from roadways is a critical operation of the DPW as it is a matter of public safety.   
The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private vendors who tirelessly 
and successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regardless of the severity or duration of a storm.  “DPW 
workers are the original Storm Troopers.” 
Cemetery 
The maintenance of the Town’s cemeteries was performed by the dedicated Cemetery Division Superintendent, Todd 
Holman, and two seasonal cemetery laborers.  The Superintendent coordinates burials with families and funeral homes 
and meets regularly with the three-member Cemetery Commission.  The Highway Division provides equipment and staff 
for maintenance and burials, which on average is forty per year.   
Wastewater 
The DPW is responsible for the day-to-day operation and maintenance of the wastewater collection system.  A specialty 
vendor is contracted to maintain, repair, and respond to emergencies at the 10 sewer pump stations.  The outstanding 
Sewer Business Manager, Barbara Lefebvre, manages the administrative functions of the Sewer Division including 
billing, customer service, permitting and budgets.  An Assistant Sewer Business Manager, Paula Bertram, was hired to 
help facilitate the many tasks of the division.  The Sewer Business Manager and DPW Director worked closely with the 
Sewer Commission to ensure sewer rules, regulations, and policies are followed and to assure the future long-term 
operation of the wastewater collection system. 
Fleet Maintenance 
The skilled and dedicated DPW Mechanic, James Sticklor, is responsible for the preventative maintenance and repair of 
all Town vehicles.  The vehicles include over 30 pieces of highway construction equipment, high performance police 
vehicles, Fire and Rescue first responder apparatus, mowers and more.  Highly technical repairs and repairs that cannot 
be completed in-house are outsourced to specialty vendors as needed.  A fleet maintenance software program continues 
to be a valuable tool as each repair is logged by vehicle identification number and stored in a comprehensive database 
and required services, repair costs over the life of the equipment, and part numbers are easily obtained. 
 
 
===PAGE 62===
62 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
Facilities Management 
 
The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:  
 
 
 
The well experienced, skilled, and dedicated Facilities Superintendent, Kit Walker, responded to alarm calls and emergencies at all 
public buildings within the department’s care 24 hours a day and 365 days per year.  A Facilities Technician, Donald Gagne, was 
hired to provide the additional support needed for the daily repair and maintenance of the Town ’s buildings.  The division keeps all 
buildings compliant with current codes to minimize interruptions of service at Town buildings so as not to disrupt or disable  essential 
services provided to the community.  When necessary, the work is outsourced to vendors including HVAC technicians, electricia ns, 
plumbers, carpenters, painters, and the like. 
The division began planning the projects on the approved Fiscal Year 2022 Capital Plan including:  
 Chiller Replacement at the Public Safety Complex 
 ADA Ramp Construction at the Ritter Building

Parks 
In conjunction with the five elected Park Commissioners and the Recreation Director, the DPW staff oversees and 
maintains all parks and grounds exclusive of school property.  The grass mowing, landscaping, and maintenance of the 
parks was outsourced to a vendor.  Marshall Park is used by the High School baseball and softball teams, Little League, 
and the Lunenburg Phillies.  McNally Field and Fitzgerald Field are available for youth programs as well.  The Town 
Beach is staffed with employees of the Parks division during the beach season.  The Recreation Director continued to use 
an on-line permitting system for beach access.  Wallis Park remains popular for its playground and basketball courts.  
The Parks Commission hired a consultant to create the Marshall Park Master Plan. 
As the new DPW Director, I truly appreciate the warm welcome and support I have received from the Select Board, 
Town Manager, Department Heads, Staff, Commissioners, and the residents of Lunenburg I have had the pleasure to 
meet.  Working together we can continue to maintain and improve our roadways and related infrastructure to exceed the 
current and future social and economic needs of the community and to preserve a healthy, safe, and quality environment 
for the Town’s citizens and visitors.  Lastly, I thank my staff for the hard work and dedication to high quality work they 
display every day that often goes unseen and unrecognized. 
 
Respectfully, 
 
William Bernard

FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
 
Fiscal Year 2023
 
Annual Town Meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on warrant articles that 
directly impact how we manage our town. Amongst these articles is the FY23 budget, capital plan, authorizations for 
various enterprise and revolving funds, and appropriations relating to stabilization funds and reserves. Throughout the 
year the Town Manager and department heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful conversations about how 
to best serve and manage the town. While developing the budget they considered funding, contracts, and mandates, as 
well as what the community wants and needs to thrive. The Town Manager formulated a recommended budget, which 
she presented to the Finance Committee on February 17th. Over six subsequent Finance Committee meetings, 
department heads presented overviews and plans relating to their budgets and capital requests. A Public Hearing was 
held on March 31st, as required by the Town Charter, and the Finance Committee voted on recommendations pertaining 
to financially relevant articles. 
 
Armed with all the information gathered during this process, the Finance Committee recommends approval of the Town 
Manager’s omnibus budget, as outlined in this warrant. 
Overall, our financial condition is good. Revenues are stable, aided by strong collections, increases in state aid, new 
growth, and a variety of federal and state grants that the town has secured. On the expense side, while not meeting every 
need outlined by the department heads, this budget does begin to address several emerging and previously unmet needs. 
The total in requested expenditures is $49,953,531.76. While we are pleased with our current fiscal standing, we do face 
challenges ahead; especially with regard to a number of our municipal buildings, which we outline in more detail below. 
Notably, our certified free cash this year is $2,666,962, which is substantially higher than in previous years, a temporary 
benefit resulting from appropriations that were not fully expended in FY21 as a result of COVID-related restrictions and 
impacts. Town fiscal policy directs portions of free cash to accounts such as stabilization, the OPEB allocation (a fund to 
pay for future retiree benefits), and the capital plan. 
Accordingly, this year’s recommended capital plan (funded by free cash, tax revenues, and unspent capital 
appropriations from previous years) is $2,162,849. It is an unprecedented investment of available funds (without 
incurring any additional debt) into the capital infrastructure that supports our town. As of the printing of this warrant, the 
Finance Committee is seeking to answer a few specific questions pertaining to the capital plan. We will have a 
recommendation on the capital plan, Article 6, at Town Meeting on May 7. 
Other notable items: 
 For FY23, the proposed appropriation for education is $23,397,371, 56.2%% of the Town’s total budget, and a 
3.78% increase over last year. This includes our Monty Tech assessment at $1,054,376, which is a 20.4% increase 
for FY23, mainly due to an increase in enrollment from 83 to 94 students. Additionally, the town budget includes 
$1,207,421 in School Retiree Health Insurance. Fortunately, this year the town benefited from an $898,610 increase 
in Chapter 70 aid, which more than offsets the $673,617 increase in the Lunenburg School District budget. 
While our Chapter 70 increase was welcome news, the increase was partly a result of formula changes that recognized 
more low-income students at a higher tier of funding. Our vulnerable populations are of top concern for all of our 
department leaders. 
An area of concern for the Finance Committee are several personnel positions that the School District is funding through 
ESSER 2 and ESSER 3 federal grants. These grants, totaling $1,939,868 will expire in FY24. Many of these positions 
are addressing acute health, academic, and social/emotional needs that have arisen from COVID-19. Over the next two 
years the School Committee will need to determine what positions we need to maintain, and how to fund them., 
 This year’s budget supports an increase of $53,443 for the library budget to support the addition of two part-time 
personnel and additional expenses. This increase will enable the library to extend its operating hours to include 
opening on Fridays.  
●     This year’s budget continues to support long-term staffing and investment programs that we committed to in 
previous years, including an additional full-time firefighter and police officer. We are again increasing road pavement 
management funding by $50,000. We are also contributing $450,000 to the special-purpose stabilization fund, which 
should give us the option to pay cash for ambulance and fire truck replacements in coming years, as opposed to 
borrowing or using free cash. 
===PAGE 64===
64 
 This year’s budget includes a 4.71% increase in IT spending, for a total of $316,022.89. In addition, the Lunenburg 
School District has budgeted $330,514 for technology-related administration and educational expenses. In late 
FY21, the town began the process of transferring the function of IT strategy and leadership to a private firm, which 
works in close consultation with town and school department leadership. Following a comprehensive review, an 
upgrade of networking and cloud services has commenced, which will continue into FY23. Additionally, the town is 
instituting rotational upgrades of computers and peripherals throughout our operations. The town website will also 
get regular upgrades. These programs are drastically improving the quality and reliability of our IT infrastructure and 
improving our security posture. The Finance Committee was pleased with the ‘out-of-the-box’ thinking and 
interdepartmental cooperation. We have encouraged all departments to find similar synergies and opportunities for 
collaboration and cost sharing. 
 As we have highlighted in previous years, Lunenburg’s debt ratio has been identified by an independent credit-rating 
agency as the sole reason that we are unable to achieve the highest-available bond rating. The good news is that our 
total debt and our debt service are declining. Our Excludable Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 9.18%, nearly 2% below 
the 11% cap. Our Net Regular Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 1.22% in FY 23, well-below the 4% cap in our fiscal 
policy. Our debt service will continue to decline over the next two years as bonds for the Public Safety Building, 
Primary School, and Library come off the debt schedule. However, this bit of good news could be temporary. There 
are several projects currently under discussion, which if acted upon and funded by borrowing, could exceed our debt 
limits in coming years. The Municipal Building Design Committee will likely have recommendations in coming 
months pertaining to Town Hall, Ritter, and the TC Passios building. The Select Board is considering re-
development of the old Primary School property at 30 School Street. The Parks Commission is formulating a master 
plan for Marshall Park and is investigating issues with the Town Beach building. And the Lunenburg School District 
anticipates a renovation or replacement of Turkey Hill Elementary School within the next ten years. 
 The warrant includes two articles that, if approved as presented, will make progress in addressing the old Primary 
School property at 30 School Street. Article 6, the capital plan, appropriates $371,000 for hazardous materials 
abatement. Article 19 appropriates $450,000 for the demolition of the building and engineering plans for the 
property’s redevelopment into a municipal park. 
 During FY22, the town received $3,507,971 from the federal government resulting from The American Rescue Plan 
Act (ARPA). These funds are not accounted for in the FY23 budget. Recent guidance from the U.S. Treasury gives 
municipal executives (in our case the Select Board) fairly wide latitude for appropriating these funds. The Select 
Board will soon be gathering opinions and ideas pertaining to how we should use these funds. Potentially, some of 
this money could be used to offset costs relating to some of the projects mentioned above. 
Lunenburg’s overall good fiscal condition is the result of adherence to conservative fiscal policy recommended by the 
Town Manager, enacted by the Select Board, and enabled by Town Meeting appropriations. There is no doubt that we 
will face new challenges in the coming years. In addition to some of the concerns outlined above, inflation is a looming 
headwind that will likely drive-up labor, capital, and materials costs. We believe that the investments we are making 
today will give us greater flexibility and capacity to address future challenges. 
The Finance Committee thanks Town Manager Heather Lemieux and Finance Director, Karen Brochu for their tireless 
dedication and leadership throughout the year, and especially during budget season. We also thank our department heads 
and all of our municipal and school employees for their exemplary service to the citizens of Lunenburg and stewardship 
of our town. 
Lunenburg Finance Committee

HISTORICAL COMMISSION 
 
 The Lunenburg Historical Commission had a productive year.in FY2022. We researched and planned for a town 
wide survey of historic houses and buildings. This is requested from every community by the Massachusetts 
Historical Commission. 
 Informative articles were written and published in the Lunenburg Ledger. 
 The Commission advocated for the survival of 68 Elm St, which is undergoing demolition by neglect. It is a 1755 
Saltbox and barn. We also advocated for the preservation of other properties. 
 The Commission facilitated the Demolition Delay bylaw. 
 We continued work on the restoration of the Arnold Litch family burial plot, which is a joint project with the 
Cemetery Commission. The DPW authorized a ground radar search and located the lost graves of the desecrated 
cemetery. A bronze plaque acknowledging the Historical and Cemetery Commissions was purchased and will be 
placed at the Arnold/Litch Cemetery plot. 
 Work continued on the Levi Goodrich family burial plot. This is another joint project with the Cemetery 
Commission. Meetings were held on site with the DPW, Cemetery Dept. and Cemetery Commission. The plot and 
the plan was reviewed. The DPW authorized ground radar and a number of unmarked children’s graves were 
discovered. The cemetery had been neglected and desecrated, and the gravestones had been toppled. Some 
gravestones were broken and lost. Levi Goodrich’s headstone was fragmented and deteriorated with some pieces 
being lost. The Commission is recreating a replacement stone for this grave. 
 The Commission explored and voted to move forward with a veteran’s banner program in the APD. It is a proposed 
joint project with the APDC. Many communities have instituted such programs, which honors veterans by putting 
banners on telephone poles with the name and photograph of a local veteran on it. 
 The Historical Commission has started research on the history of African American slaves and Freedmen in 
Lunenburg. 
 We supported the APDC in its work to advocate for and protect the historic buildings and houses located within the 
National Historic District. 
Richard McGrath, Chair, LHC 
 
Bill Lakso, Vice Chair  
Kevin Boussom, Secretary 
Jennifer Sanderson, Member

"Whatever the cost of our libraries, the price is cheap compared to that of an ignorant 
nation." -Walter Cronkite 
As the pandemic stretched into Fiscal Year 2022, we saw lingering disruption of old patterns and new patterns of 
behavior continuing. One constant, however, seems to repeat itself: the library builds value for the community. 
The library has continued to follow its strategic plan this year. Keeping our mission close to our hearts: 
Lunenburg Public Library welcomes all and serves residents of the Lunenburg area as a hub for the community, 
enriching our lives through its collections, programs, services, facility, and people.  
Our library resources reach far into the community. You no longer need to visit the library in person to access our 
resources. The library has focused on improving and expanding the number of resources available digitally, and made 
concerted efforts to ease access to those resources. We also added Niche Academy to help patrons learn about our digital 
resources and how to access them. Another new resource that will be much appreciated is Mango Languages, offering a 
convenient way to learn over 70 foreign languages, with a focus on conversations. 
Patrons can use all of our databases, e-books and e-audiobooks, our website (lunenburglibrary.org), and even streaming 
services like Kanopy from their homes or cars or workplace. You could even attend a virtual program or interact with 
library staff for help on a question or a reading recommendation without visiting the library. Of course, if you never 
visited, you would be missing out on the beautiful building. 
You would also be missing out on one of the most important services we offer: library programs. Although attendance in
-person during FY22 was not as consistent as it was immediately before the pandemic, there are still programs 
happening at the library nearly every day, and our consistency paid dividends during the summer, as attendance at 
programs really returned to a normal level. During the year library staff focused on providing access to programs 
virtually and in-person, and continuing to build a dependable, consistent calendar of events that would appeal to a variety 
of people.  
Library staff have also focused on trying to find new ways for residents to engage with our library collection. 
Particularly worthy of note are curated experiences that have been very popular. Individuals even travel from nearby 
communities to try out these kits. Our Adult Services Librarian, Lydia Gravel, introduced the idea with “Mystery Travel 
Kits” which create an enriching learning experience mimicking some parts of travelling to a foreign country by using 
items in the library collection. Each kit typically contains several books, a movie or two, music, and some activities or 
games. These kits are still being curated and can be requested through our online event calendar on our website.  
As the year went on, we saw more and more people actually visiting the library. Each afternoon during the school year, 
we have been seeing more students than ever before. Partnering with the Boys and Girls Club brings dozens of students 
to study during the afternoon Power Hour. In addition to that, we often see dozens of other teens, spending time at the 
library. They play video games, study quietly, eat snacks, and sometimes make a little too much noise. We are, however, 
excited that they have chosen to come to the library after school. 
We continue to focus on stewarding the facility for the future. Efficiency and sustainability are priorities for the trustees 
and the director. This year we completed replacement of a number of lighting fixtures and bulbs, so that we are now 
almost completely lit by LEDs. The HVAC system has also been at the forefront of our attention, as it is beginning to 
show the signs of its age. Increased maintenance needs are typical of an aging system, and ours is no exception. We will 
continue to make responsible plans for the facility, so that it will be a gem for Lunenburg for decades to come.

 We launched 4 reading challenges for adults, using Beanstack logging software to record: 
 66 readers who joined in at least one challenge, reading more than 245,733 minutes 
 683 badges were earned for specific goals or milestones within the challenges  
 We hosted 105 programs and meetings for our adult patrons and 1,040 people attended. 
 13 of these events were held virtually, mostly on the Zoom meeting platform.

Adult Service Goals for FY23 & Beyond 
1. Identify areas of programming that should be developed with greater intention, and experiment with new ideas to 
keep events fresh. 
2. Cultivate deeper partnerships with local institutions and groups, including offsite programs, thereby becoming a more 
recognizable and connected resource in our community. 
3. Encourage meaningful social interactions between patrons at the library through the introduction of new programs 
such as book clubs, open mics, craft circles, or support groups.

 We learned of our L S T A Grant Award which will arrive in FY2023 and will be used in the Children’s Area to

kits will be available for patron and classroom use. 
 We purchased and installed the BEAM projector system, thanks to the Friends of the Lunenburg Public Library. 
Something you don’t often see in libraries, especially those our size, this interactive learning game system comes 
with 100 games designed to engage and entertain players of all ages. The games provide education reinforcement 
and highly social activities.

specifically designed to provide educational resources and reinforcement activities for elementary aged students. 
 We offered 117 programs for children, attended by 2,409 individuals. The include storytimes, book clubs, Lego 
nights, and so many more! 
 We added a raised bed Children’s Garden. This garden features a variety of herbs and plants chosen to provide 
sensory and educational experiences for library patrons of all ages. 
 We completed a successful Summer Reading Program to help bridge the gap between school years. We registered 
221 children as participants, and offered 57 programs which were attended by 1240 children and family members 
during the program. 
Children’s Service Goals for FY23 & Beyond 
1. Encourage children and families to use the library from an early age as this will make them more likely to remain 
users in future years. 
2. Continue to provide programs especially geared toward engaging students in grades 4-6. 
3. Add new book club for kids in grades 2-3 based on “Choose Your Own Adventure Series.” 
4. Provide STEM programs and circulating STEM kits for children ages 5-11.

Looking forward to FY2023 
One thing to really get excited about is our award of Library Services and Technology Act (LSTA) grant 
funding to support STEM learning for all ages during FY2023. We hope to bring programming, circulating 
kits, learning guides, and more to people of all ages with this funding. Additionally, having secured funding 
for two additional staff members, we anticipate opening our doors to the public Friday afternoons, increasing 
the number of hours during the week that the public can access the library by 11%. These big changes will 
positively impact the library’s ability to serve our community and enable us to reach groups of residents who 
might not normally have the opportunity to engage with library services. We will plan and implement outreach 
services and an expanded program plan to continue to deliver these important core library services. We plan 
for the library to continue to be a vital, thriving location where people of all ages can come to meet, learn, and 
have fun. Come visit the library soon!
