LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)  
 
 
 
 
 
FY22  Mission: Establish, develop, modernize and operate the Town of Lunenburg’s  
Public, Government, and Educational (PEG) Access Channels to reflect what matters to  
Lunenburg 
Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) 
Steve Walker, Chair 
Pete Lincoln, Vice Chair 
Steve Archambault, Secretary 
Faith Beall, Member 
Paul Sarapas, Member 
 
The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the Public 
Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town’s only cable-service provider. 
 
The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-broadcast 
programming from both town, school functions and meetings, as well as programming from publicly available sources. 
 
Programming 
CH9 - Public 
Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 63% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY22, down 7% from FY21.  The total 
number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (outside resources), decreased from 91 in FY21 to 82 
programs in FY22. 
The 52 local programs included high school sports, band concerts, school events and featured projects. The remaining 30 
(37%) programs were obtained through shared media sources. 
 
Channel 9 – Public Channel 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Several categories of local programming were aired on Ch9. Among the top programs were Informational 28%, Events 
23% and Entertainment 22%, making up the majority 63% of Ch9 programming (see chart, below). 
 
Two of the top three program categories, Informational and Events (51%) provided viewers with presentations and 
overviews that helped to familiarize them with resources and information, as well as current events happening around 
town. 

===PAGE 70===
70 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Locally produced programs and government meetings are available on the LunenburgAccess YouTube Channel.  http://
www.youtube.com/LunenburgAccess 
 
Links to the YouTube channel for local Ch9 programs can be found on Lunenburg Public Access’ Facebook Page 
https://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable 
 
CH8 – Government 
Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally broadcast meetings.  In addition to several live weekly 
municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other examples of government programs downloaded from MassAccess 
(massaccess.telvue.com), include Massachusetts State Agency PSAs and programs of a political nature.  There were also 
additional programs from other access stations added, including the Kushmeric Connection from Fitchburg Public 
Access. 
 
FY22 brought a slight increase in Government programming with more committee meeting and event recording requests. 
The additional programming allowed residents to view meetings they normally wouldn’t see.  And during this time, PAC 
also improved the recording of Town Meeting using 4 cameras, which increased the overall quality of the broadcast. 
 
A few committees and boards continue to use Zoom only, (not live broadcast) and record meetings to the Cloud. These 
meetings are uploaded from the Cloud to one of the Lunenburg Public Access YouTube channels (LunenburgAccess) for 
later viewing. This has resulted in easy access to a greater number of board and committee meetings. 
 
CH99 – Educational 
In May of FY20, PAC added an Educational Channel, Comcast Channel 99.  The additional channel has allowed PAC to 
feature local educational content to viewers in addition to the bi-monthly School Committee meetings.  As of FY22, 
most of the channel’s content remains to be provided by download resources, including programs such as “Auto Sense”, 
“Fur, Fins and Feathers”, and “Chess Chat.” 
 
PAC continues to work toward content growth on this channel. 
 
As a member of the MassAccess organization, PAC 
is given access to the Telvue Connect 
(massaccess.telvue.com) database for sharing 
productions of both state and nationwide 
programming.  Many shows downloaded and aired on Channel 9 and Ch99 are sourced from this outlet, including 
“Democracy NOW”, “Off the Shelf”, and “In the Toybox. “  
 
In FY20, PAC began sharing their own locally produced programs with the MassAccess content database.  The results 
were outstanding!  In FY22, Lunenburg’s “Talkin’ Baseball” episodes were, and continue to be, downloaded by over 60 
media centers around the country.  And, “Storytime – The Books  We Love” was downloaded by over 70 public access  

===PAGE 71===
71 
stations mostly located in Massachusetts but reaching as far as California.  We are delighted to showcase our programs 
to the community while using the database to add to PAC content. 
 
In addition to MassAccess Telvue downloads, PAC also receives local programs from nearby access stations that air on 
Ch9, including “Barbara and You”, “Chess Chat” and “Wicked Good Food.” 
PAC Manager, staff and committee members regularly attend MassAccess conferences and Meet & Greets to develop 
relationships and resources with other media centers. 
 
Studio and Equipment 
PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013.  We encourage the public to take advantage of the 
facilities and equipment offered for creative productions. The PAC manager also supports various town departments in 
the use of public access’ resources for their communication needs. 
In FY22 PAC made a major investment adding new studio set lighting.  With the addition of professional lighting, 
programs saw a drastic increase in quality.   PAC videographers were trained on the use of the new lighting and have the 
experience and know-how to provide professional quality programming. 
As the need for multi-camera productions has increased, PAC added a new AV MATRIX portable switcher.  This 
switcher was used on location for Town Meetings as well as sports and events.  It replaced the outdated switcher which 
was complicated and cumbersome. 
 
Staff, Producers and Volunteers 
In FY22, there was one full-time Manager and 5 part-time contract videographers and a contracted technical consultant.  
There were openings for 2 part-time videographers. 
The PAC Manager administers the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all production and programming 
schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, studio and inventory administration, advertising, social 
media and equipment and system troubleshooting.  Videographers provided video recording of committee meetings and 
additional local video assignments and projects.   
A technical contract consultant worked with the PAC manager to provided technical and training assistance as needed.  
It is expected that this contract will continue through FY23.  However, PAC searched for, and has the funds budgeted to 
hire a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the staff in FY23. 
PAC is always looking for producers.  If a community member has a special skill or interest, PAC will help make the 
show happen.  Our studio and staff are equipped to assist new producers in video production. 
Much effort has gone into recruiting volunteers, with little success.  
 
Financials 
PAC received its income from Comcast in quarterly payments (received August, November, February and May) and 
monthly interest payments deposited into the Enterprise Fund. The overall revenue received for FY22 from Comcast was 
$199,956.37 staying flat from $199,736.30 (+0 .11 % over FY21).  The budget starting balance was set at $220,866 for 
FY22 projected expenses and capital costs.

Summary 
FY22 was a recovery year coming out of the pandemic of FY20 and FY21.  The new “normal” has become standard 
practice.  Zoom Hybrid meetings continue to be administered with new on-location Zoom Hybrid environments being 
planned for boards and committees.  PAC continued to administer these meetings and has adapted to the new 
technology.  Hybrid meetings have allowed PAC to air more Government meetings than ever before.   
While overall programming did not increase over FY21, PAC made a few key equipment improvements during this 
period.  The addition of a new camera switcher made an impact on the production of on-location broadcasts and multi-
camera shoots.  Sports and events were improved due to the streamlined switcher.  And in the studio, the addition of new 
lighting has been a “game-changer” bringing our programs to a more professional level.  These upgrades will pave the 
way for better productions and content in FY23.   
Won’t you join us as a producer or volunteer?  PAC’s supportive staff offers production assistance, 
making it easy to produce your own show. Our community is thriving with talented and creative individuals – now is 
your time to shine!  If producing doesn’t appeal to you, then consider “behind the camera” options.  Become a volunteer. 
Volunteers are the “life blood” of media centers around the state, and without that involvement PAC will not be able to 
grow.  Consider volunteering to help provide the necessary resources needed for PAC’s continued success.   
We need you! 
SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM 
JANUARY 1, 2022 – NOVEMBER 1, 2022 
The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is administered by the Assistant Town Manager/HR Director. 
What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off? 
 
The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to have an 
abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing jobs such as general 
office work, custodial, library shelving, meals on wheels, school assistance, etc., to earn the tax abatement. 
The 2022 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to the participant’s 3rd quarter real estate tax bill. The current 
hourly rate is $15.00 per hour. 
 
Eligibility Requirements 
 Applicants must be 60 years of age or older 
 Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1st 
 Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse’s name (60 years+) 
 Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):  
     one person household - $50,000   
     two person household - $75,000  
 Earnings are exempt from state taxes 
 Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Retirement 
or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement 
 Final amount earned will be applied to the 3rd quarter real estate tax  
 All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check  
 All Property Tax Workers must re- apply and be accepted annually for participation in the program

SEWER COMMISSION – FY22 
The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the future, and 
developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town’s Charter, responsibility for the day-to-
day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager who 
delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational side of the system to the Department of Public 
Works Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 86% of Lunenburg 
sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 14% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg. 
In FY22 the following members served on the Sewer Commission; Dave MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair, 
Brett Ramsden-Clerk and Members Mike Deveikis and Mike Nault. The Commission continued to meet virtually via 
Zoom for all meetings and voted to begin each meeting with the Pledge of Allegiance.  
There are currently 915 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 327 which have access to Town 
Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY22, 14 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The Commission billed out 
$1,021,525 in quarterly sewer bills and had $30,593 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners’ property tax bills 
for non-payment.    
During FY22, CTE (Chad Empey) continued to provide excellent service for the Operation & Maintenance of the Sewer 
Stations. The Commission approved funds for repairs to the Dana Street Station which included the replacement of the 
impellers, seals and gaskets and approved the replacement of the electrical panel at the Mass 2 station.  These upgrades 
are important to keep the stations running smoothly. The Commission voted 3/8/22 to approve the Year 3 contract with 
CTE, with a 4% increase to commence 7/1/22. 
DPW Director Rob Oliva worked with the Commission in FY22 to implement the Maintenance Plan for the sewer 
system. Clean Basins was contracted to camera and auger a blockage on Mass Ave. near Riley Road. In October 2021 
Green Mountain Pipe grouted the pipe joints on Elizabeth St. to address a long-standing Inflow/Infiltration (I/I) issue in 
3 services. Rob unfortunately left Lunenburg in March 2022, but the Commission welcomed Bill Bernard in May as 
Director.   
===PAGE 75===
75 
SEWER COMMISSION – FY22 
Bill has been working diligently with the Commission to carry out the many Maintenance Plan projects in process, such 
as jetting, manhole repairs and overseeing maintenance at the pump stations.  
In March 2022 the Commission approved hiring an Assistant Business Manager, to share the increasing demands of the 
Business Manager’s job.  Paula Bertram was hired to assist the Business Manager, Barb Lefebvre with billing, account 
management and other office activities.    
In FY22, the Commission continued the annual permit program for the 20 Food Service Establishments with grease traps 
and interceptors on Town Sewer. The Commission once again voted to waive the Annual Permit Renewal Fee for the 
Grease Trap Program, as many restaurants were still struggling to gain back business after the Covid 19 closures. In 
February 2022, the Commission voted to drop the Annual Permit Renewal fees for establishments in compliance, 
effective with FY23 renewals.  Those who are not in compliance will pay $350 for internal traps and $650 for external 
trap renewals.  Grease Trap compliance continues to be a focus of the Commission as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can 
negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the sewer system.  While the Grease Trap Regulation 
focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease 
into the system which increases everyone’s costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.  
While working on the FY23 budget, an 8% rate increase was approved by the Commission to cover increasing costs in 
usage from Fitchburg and Leominster. The rate increased from $12.65/100 cu.ft. to $13.66/100 cu.ft. and the minimum 
charge from $107.00 to $115.00/quarter.  The Commission continued working in FY22 with Flow Assessment to 
maintain and ensure the accuracy of the flow meters that measure usage going to Fitchburg and Leominster.  
The Commission also voted to update several policies for re-connection fees, prorating bills for change of ownership or 
new connections, easements and waivers. A public hearing will be held in FY23 to address the policy updates.  
The Commission met with several groups in FY22 looking to expand sewer. The Parks Commission requested to expand 
the Sewer Service Area (SSA) to include Marshall Park, which was approved at the November 2021 Town Meeting. 
Extensions for Upland Ave./Eastern Ave. and White St./Maple Parkway were discussed and preliminary designs, with 
estimates, were done by Wright Pierce. However, a survey of residents did not indicate the necessary 2/3rds interest in 
favor of the extensions. Other projects before the Commission  were an 8-unit condo at 711 Mass. Ave., lots  at 790-792 
Mass. Ave., and a marijuana establishment (Uma Flowers) at 43 Summer St.  A horse rescue facility and 20-unit 
residential project at 5 Chestnut St. requested to be added to the SSA. An article was added to the May Town Meeting 
but was passed over. The 200 apartment 40B project application on Howard Street was approved by Mass Housing to 
proceed in seeking approvals from the Lunenburg ZBA.  The project will seek a large expansion of the Sewer Service 
Area and Water District to bring utilities to the project.  
At the 5/7/22 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 4 articles, 3 of which related to the FY23 budget and 
stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY23, $853,112.53 was appropriated from Sewer Enterprise 
revenues, $20,000.00 transferred from the Sewer Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund, $20,962.00 was transferred from 
the Sewer Reserve Capacity Fund and the sum of $319,107.47 was appropriated from Retained Earnings for a total 
appropriation of $1,213,182.00 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY23, including the amount of $793,968.47 
approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund’s share of Town services and debt service on the sewer betterments. The 
second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $35,548.86, approximately

VETERANS’  SERVICES 
The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has the most comprehensive system of assistance offered to Veterans, 
families, and surviving spouses in all of the United States.  Every city or town in Massachusetts, in accordance 
with chapter 115 of the Massachusetts General Laws, must have available to its citizens a Veterans’ Services 
Officer (VSO), either full- or part-time, as determined by the community population, in order to serve the 
needs of its Veterans.  Lunenburg has a part-time VSO at the Ritter Building at 960 Massachusetts Ave.  
Working with the Town census of 2021, shows Lunenburg in excess of 618 residents who claimed either 
military service or veteran status.  On a monthly basis, the veterans and widows of veterans in Lunenburg 
receive in excess of $328,330.74 in federal benefits (Veterans Administration), which they mostly put back 
into the community by paying their taxes and through discretionary spending.  
Veterans, families, or widows who qualify can be provided monthly financial assistance (reimbursements) for 
specific living expenses such as heating fuel, medical care, and pharmaceutical co-payments.  With the VSO’s 
approval, the Town of Lunenburg provides the financial assistance and is then reimbursed by the state’s 
department of Veterans’ Services (DVS).  It is the function of the Veterans’ Services Office to identify 
qualifying members of the community and assist with enrollment into the DVS program.  Additionally, certain 
Veterans and/or widows may also be eligible for federal benefits from the Veterans Administration (VA).  
Only Massachusetts offers both of these services to every community.   
During Fiscal Year 2022 (July 2021- June 2022), Lunenburg served an average of 10 recipients (Veterans, 
families, or widows) per month.  Lunenburg paid out $64,435.43 in benefits, or an average of $5,369.62 per 
recipient per month.  Lunenburg was reimbursed $48,326.57 by DVS, which is 75% of what was given as 
assistance, the maximum allowable share. 
Some examples of Veterans’ Service Office assistance services include: 
 Enrollment in chapter 115, State Veterans benefits 
 Assistance obtaining military service records or replacement medals/decorations 
 Tuition waivers at Massachusetts state colleges 
 Housing assistance, including Veterans Shelters and Veterans Homes 
 Payments of “Welcome Home” bonuses for deployed Veterans 
 Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates 
 Employment assistance 
The Veterans’ Service Office also provides information, advice, and assistance to any citizen interested in 
learning about their qualifications for federal benefits, such as: 
 VA disability payments and property tax exemptions 
 Tax-exempt annuities for parents or spouses of those killed in Action or those who are declared 100% 
disabled from service-connected conditions. 
 VA pensions, Aid and Attendance assistance, and death benefits 
 VA medical care for both physical and mental needs 
 Vocational and Educational opportunities (the GI Bill) 
 VA Loans 
 Burial Assistance and Grave Markers (Veterans or Civilian cemeteries) 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
David Lauzon Jr. 
Interim VSO 
Office phone 978-582-4176

BOARD OF HEALTH 
The Board of Health Office is located in the Ritter Building on the corner of Mass. Ave and Lancaster Ave. Our office is 
on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.  We can be reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.  
The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its mission to 
maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2022, reviewing Title V Inspection Reports, building 
permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public health 
related issues as well as resident concerns.    
In addition, our local Board continued to be tasked with responding to the emerging COVID-19 pandemic.  The Board of 
Health assisted residents and businesses in understanding ever-changing guidance and compliance with those orders.  We 
continued to conducted compliance checks and responded to complaints.  As the pandemic continued, our office assisted 
residents and businesses in understanding testing, quarantine and necessary compliance.  Through Nashoba Associated 
Boards of Health, regional Covid Vaccine Clinics were held.  Wash hands frequently, wear a mask, and keep six feet rule. 
Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing.  Meetings continue between our agents, other 
Town Departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our 
region.   
This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, repairs or 
upgrades to septic systems, well permits and food service permits.  The Board continues its vigilance with regard to 
septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect the 
environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.   
The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba Associated 
Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, nursing services is provided to our resident.  This year, 
clinics provided our residents with inoculations for Flu as well as Senior well adult clinics.  
The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project.  Mosquito Control monitors the 
mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays 
frequently to control the population during the warmer times of the year.  Residents who want to have their property 
sprayed can do so by emailing to:  cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage 
at:  http://www.cmmcp.org , or calling the Board of Health office.  This service is free of charge to residents. 
With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to ill residents recovering from illness and 
Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.  
Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.     
Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of Lunenburg. 
If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board office at 582 -4146 
ext. 430.

Nashoba Nursing Service & Hospice 
Nashoba’s Certified Home Health Registered Nurses visit and provide skilled services to patients in their home under 
physician’s orders.  These services include assessment of physical, psychological, and nutritional needs. Skills include 
teaching/supervision of medications, wound care, ostomy care, IV therapy on a 24 -hour basis, catheter care, case 
management and referral to other services as needed. 
Nashoba’s Certified Home Care Aides provide assistance with daily activities of daily living including bathing dressing, 
exercises and meal preparation. 
Nashoba Therapists provide skilled physical, occupational, speech, and dietary therapeutic interventions through assessment, 
treatment and education.  Their integration of client, caregiver, and medical outcomes aims at attaining maximum functional 
dependence.

Community Health- Communications and Public Health Education Summary 
The Public Health Educator/Communications Specialist (PHE/CS) role has evolved since its inception in Sept 2021 to 
best support the needs of the District . In the past year, the PHE/CS has been communicating timely and relevant public 
health information to towns and stakeholders, and providing educational opportunities across the District with a social 
determinants of health (SDOH) lens.  This work has included: 
 Developing educational programs and presentations both independently and collaboratively with the PH nursing 
team- Harm reduction, emergency preparedness, etc. 
 Providing education and advocacy related to treatment and de-stigmatization of substance use disorder 
 Maintaining up-to-date distribution lists, communicating within the district via channels such as quarterly 
newsletters, regular email updates, etc. pertaining to public health news, information and events such as flu and 
rabies clinics.  Exploring ways to diversify communication methods to reach as many community members as 
possible. 
 Building and maintaining relationships with stakeholders through meetings and attending community events to 
represent NABH 
 Serving as a resource for community members with public health related questions across a broad range of topics; 
developing a robust, in-depth Resources page for the website (which is currently being finalized) 
 Attending trainings with an emphasis on health equity and public health service regionalization.  MDPH has 
prioritized these areas because research has shown they have the biggest impact on health outcomes for the broadest 
range of people. 
 PHE/CS has utilized an SDOH lens to identify health inequities that most impact the NABH region to focus efforts 
and collaborate with community partners to lessen these gaps. 
 Developing educational and communications materials for district and individual towns by request including print, 
electronic, presentation, and TV/news media materials 
 Maintaining and improving upon NABH website to keep info relevant and accessible. Maintaining agency social 
media to reach more people in district with current, relevant and seasonal public health information. 
 Organizing events to facilitate the exchange of information such as a Spring Wellness Festival and first regional in     
formal local board of health gathering

FIRE DEPARTMENT 
The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing 
the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg. 
 
In 2022, your Fire Department responded to 1990 calls for service, broken down as follows:  
Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions        385                Emergency Medical      1143 
Inspections                  446                 Other                             16 
This represents a 14% increase in emergency calls for service over 2021, mostly in Emergency Medical calls.  
The Department consists of 10 Career and 30 -35 On Call/Per Diem Firefighters and Emergency Medical 
Technicians, plus a full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator.  Three Firefighter/EMT or Paramedics are on 
duty 24/7 at the Station.  This coverage is provided by a combination of our Career Staff and Per Diem Firefighters.  
Staffing the Station with dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency 
occurs.  As many calls require more personnel than are on duty, we continue to rely heavily on the dedication and 
commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. 
After nearly five years of preparation, the Department is in the final stages of licensing to provide our own 
Paramedic Service.  Firefighter Olivia Hayes certified as a Paramedic in October 2022 and Firefighter Melissa Buchanan 
will be obtaining her certification in January 2023. This brings us to four Fulltime Firefighter/Paramedics with an 
additional eight On Call/per diem Firefighter/Paramedics.  In addition, Firefighter Jennie Smith will begin training as a 
Paramedic in January of 2023.  Paramedic training is in excess of 2000 hours and requires anywhere from 9 -18 months 
of college level classroom education, clinical time and field internships to obtain certification.  The Department 
continues to train our newly hired full time staff to this level and recruit licensed Paramedics to fill Career positions 
when possible.  We will begin providing Paramedic Level care to the community in January 2023.  
In 2022, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 446 Inspections.   This is in addition to multiple fire drills and 
public education initiatives.  By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations 
when a home is sold.  We are also required to inspect all new oil burner and propane gas installations.  Any business 
holding a liquor license is also requires a yearly inspection for fire safety issues.  In addition, all new construction 
projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction 
process.  Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2022 include: 
 Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety Education in 
both the Schools and with Senior Citizens.  This Grant provides funding for both materials and personnel to bring 
fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.   
 Participation in the Senior Center ’s “Sand for Seniors” Program.  This program provides buckets of walkway sand 
for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months.  In partnership with this 
program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for Smoke 
and Carbon Monoxide Detectors.  If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to install free detectors 
obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.    
 It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke detectors. 
 Firefighters Samuel Johnson and Timothy O ’Connell graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting 
Academy’s Call/Volunteer Recruit Training Program in June of 2022.  This 240 -hour program trains new personnel in 
the basics of firefighting and hazardous materials response.  The program is a requirement for any new Firefighters with 
the Department.   
Eight Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive 
Teams.  These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such as Confined Space, Rescue from 
Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery.  In conjunction with 
these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer.   
Apparatus replacement has reached a plateau for a few years, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck all under 
10 years old.  The Ambulances continue to see increased wear and tear as we see not only increased calls for service, but 
longer transport times to other hospitals and critical care facilities.  It should be noted that 30 years ago, we had four 
hospitals within a 10 -15 minute transport time. Today there are two.  In addition, many cardiac and trauma patients 
require definitive treatment in specialty facilities, often requiring transports to Worcester.  Given long delays in 
obtaining new units, a replacement for Rescue 1, our 2014 ambulance has been moved up two years and is being 
requested on the FY’24 Capital Plan.  
===PAGE 85===
85 
FIRE DEPARTMENT 
The Turnout Gear Replacement program that was begun under the Capital Budget last year has been completed.  
All personnel now have state of the art protective clothing, with a serviceable spare set of equipment as well.  
Firefighting protective clothing must be cleaned after every fire to rid it of cancer causing materials that are often 
byproducts of today’s synthetic materials burning.  As washing and drying firefighting cloth can take from 24 -48 hours, 
having a spare set available allows all personnel to remain “in service” at all time. 
Replacement of our outdated Forest Fire protective clothing with a dual -purpose type of clothing that is suitable 
for both brush fires and motor vehicle/technical rescue situations is still underway.  This project is funded through a 
State Grant for Safety Equipment.  Though supply chain issues have slowed the process, we anticipate having the 
equipment by spring of 2023.  A State Firefighting Safety Grant was also received in 2022.  This will replace several 
hundred feet of fire hose and some outdated nozzles and adapters.  This grant is expected to be completed sometime in 
2023. 
In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding 
commitment to the Town.  Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress me every day.  In 
addition, I would like to express my gratitude to the members of the Department who have helped make the Paramedic 
Program a reality.  From those who helped develop policies and those two helped procure equipment, to those members 
who endured the challenges of Paramedic training.  Your efforts have helped bring about a massive improvement in 
service to our residents.  My thanks also to the Town Manager for her unwavering support of this vital project. 
I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help.  The 
level of interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business in invisible to many people, but it 
is critical to all operations.  Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing support of 
public safety.  Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have come to expect from us.

      Values: 
 We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty. 
 We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the 
community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department. 
 We are a proactive and problem solving agency. 
 We strive to serve our citizens in a professional manner with compassion, respect, and empathy. 
 We are committed to providing the highest quality of police services to our community. 
 We recognize the value of our employees, and recognize they are our most valued resource. We will strive to 
provide our employees with the necessary resources, training, and career development to enhance their 
professionalism. 
Mission Statement:  
We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services 
to our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the benefits that 
are realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed 
with our community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship. We will strive to deliver 
police services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all 
individual rights. We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend 
criminal offenders, and help resolve quality of life issues.

Department Review: 
It is an honor to again provide the Lunenburg Police Department’s Annual report as the Chief of Police. 2022 continued 
to be a challenging year for the Police Department as the community came out of the grips of Covid–19. We have also 
faced the challenges associated with the economy, population growth, and increased traffic within the community. A 
nationwide trend of recruiting and retaining Police Officers continue to have an effect on our Department. Although 
faced with these challenges, the Department remains committed to being pro- active, and strives to make the Community 
safe for all. 
 
K9 Jerry will be retiring at the end of the fiscal year. Jerry has been with the Department since 2014, and has performed 
admirably throughout his career. Jerry will remain with Sgt. Tocci, who has agreed to keep him through his retirement 
years. We wish Jerry good health and a long retirement. Job well done Jerry. 
 
Off. Nicholas Marino accepted a position with the Acton Police Department. We thank Nick for his service, and wish 
him the best of luck with Acton PD. 
 
Reserve Officer/Desk Officer Lisa Melnicki has accepted a position in the private sector. We thank Lisa for her service, 
and wish her the best of luck in her future endeavors. 
 
Off. Jesse Reed was hired to the full times ranks. We are happy to welcome a veteran police officer from Berlin Police 
Department who will bring his experience and knowledge to the Department. 
 
Off. Deven O’Brien has returned to the Department as a full time police officer. Off. O’Brien started his career with our 
Department as a Reserve Officer in 2013 and in 2014 was appointed as a full time police officer. In 2018 Off. O’Brien 
accepted a position with the Marlborough Police Department. We were happy to welcome Off. O’Brien back to the 
Department, and continue his service where his Law Enforcement career began. 
 
Off. Paul Theodoulou was hired to the full time ranks after serving a short period time as a Reserve Officer with the 
Department. Off. Theodoulou is a veteran police officer from Fitchburg Police Department as well Townsend Police 
Department. Off. Theodoulou brings a wealth of experience and knowledge to our Department. We were fortunate to 
have landed Off. Theodoulou. 
 
Off. George “Trey” Tibbetts was elevated to a full time position after serving a short period of time as a Reserve Officer 
with the Department. Off. Tibbetts was added to the Department in accordance with the Department’s 10 year hiring 
plan. Trey completed his training in a relatively short period of time, and we are excited to watch him grow with the 
Department. 
 
As we became fully staffed we anticipated filling our vacant positions. The first and foremost was filling a vacant 
Sergeant’s position that would provide us much needed supervision for the Patrol Bureau. We also anticipated filling a 
vacant Detective’s position, as well as adding an additional School Resource/Community Outreach Officer. 
 
Sgt. John Morreale was promoted to fill the vacant Sergeant’s position. Sgt. Morreale has been with the Department 
since 2014 when he began his career as a Reserve Officer, and has worked up through the ranks as he has progressed in 
his career. Sgt. Morreale has been assigned as the Department’s Accreditation Manager as well as the split shift patrol 
supervisor. 
 
Det. Joshua Mathieu was elevated to the Detective position. Det. Mathieu started with the Department in 2011 and has 
risen through the ranks to his current position. Det. Mathieu brings a wealth of experience, knowledge and training to the 
position. Det. Mathieu is an excellent investigator, and will be a great addition to the Investigative Bureau. 
 
On October 26, 2022 the Department was certified through the Massachusetts Police Accreditation Commission 
(MPAC), which is recognized as a best practice organization. Through the tireless work of Sgt. Morreale, and the 
cooperation and collaboration with members of our Department we were able to complete the first phase of 
accreditation. The Department was recognized and received the MPAC award on October 26, 2022 at the Commission’s 
===PAGE 89===
89 
award ceremony held that Marlborough Country Club. We look forward to an accelerated process and becoming fully 
accredited by the summer of 2023. 
We continue to work towards a Co-Response Model approach to mental health type of calls. This would involve 
partnering officer(s) with a mental health clinician to assist with individuals in crisis. We have been meeting with 
Community Health Link and Advocates to assess our needs, and develop a Co-Response plan. We have also been 
exploring a partnership with the Town of Lancaster to share a clinician between the towns. We hope to have this 
program become a reality by the fall of 2023. 
2022 Statistical Information: 
The overall goal of the Lunenburg Police Department is a reduction of crime, assure quality of life issues are 
addressed, and provide a safe community to live, work and visit. The Department evaluates effectiveness, in part, 
by statistical information that is generated through the Records Management System.

===PAGE 90===
90 
As result of the above calls our officers generated a total of 1,777 written reports. There were 434 reports 
involving Incident Based Reporting (IBR), and National Incident Based Reporting (NIBRS), which is a State and 
Federal Crime reporting data base. The Officers also generated an additional 483 written reports that did not fall 
into the IBR/NIBRS category. 
According to NIBRS, reported crimes are broken down into three categories, Crimes Against Persons, Crimes Against 
Property, and Crimes Against Society. These are further broken down into Group A and Group B categories to closely 
match the Uniform Crime Reporting (UCR) program. The Lunenburg Police Department regularly reports IBR 
information to the State, which in turn reports the information to the Federal Bureau of Investigation. 
In 2022 there has been a 19% increase in Group A, Crimes Against Persons, with Aggravated and Simple Assaults 
as well as Intimidation (witness/victim) accounting for the majority of the incidents with 39 out of 62 total reported 
incidents. These numbers appear to have a direct correlation to the Domestic Violence calls that officers have 
responded to in 2022. 
In 2022 there has been a 34% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Property. Although we have observed a 
decrease in property crimes, the area we have observed an increase is shoplifting, accounting for a majority of the 
incidents with 40 out of 142 total reported incidents. 
Training: 
Training continues to be a priority for the Department. The Department’s in-house trainers look for ingenuitive ways to 
complement our annual in-service training. In addition, several officers have attended specialized training in various 
areas of Law Enforcement as a part of professional development. 
 
Use of Force: 
The Department conducts an annual analysis of Use of Force incidents, as well as review(s) of the incidents as they 
occur. In 2022 officers were involved in nine (9) use of force incidents, which remained the same as compared to 2021. 
In each Use of Force incident de-escalation tactics, including the use of verbal tactics, were first utilized prior to any Use 
of Force application. Officers continue to strive to de-escalate confrontations prior to any application of Use of Force. 
 
Motor Vehicle Pursuits: 
The Department conducts an annual analysis of Motor Vehicle Pursuits, as well as review(s) of pursuits as they occur. In 
2022 officers were involved in five (5) motor vehicle pursuits, which remained the same as compared to 2021. One 
pursuit resulted in a motor vehicle accident but occurred after the pursuit had been terminated. No injuries to the public, 
officers or suspect(s) involved in the pursuits were reported. 
 
Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District: 
The Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District (NVRDD) is a Regional Emergency Communications Center located in 
Devens, MA. NVRDD serves as the 9 -1-1 public safety answering point and dispatch center for Police, Fire and EMS 
for the communities of Lunenburg, Bolton, Devens, Harvard, Lancaster and Lunenburg. NVRDD was one of the first 
Regional Emergency Communications Centers in the State of Massachusetts, opening its doors on June 18, 2013. 
NVRDD serves a total population of just over 35,000 residents and thousands more visitors to the orchards, farms, 
businesses, and attractions within its six communities. In 2022, NVRDD handled 33,500 phone calls coming in on 911 
and business lines. NVRDD also coordinated the response to over 89,900 calls for service. NVRDD's staff are highly 
skilled in call-handling and dispatching, and are all able to perform both functions simultaneously. These first of the first
-responders provide a vital and seamless link between the community and their local police, fire, and emergency medical 
services. While the focus is on handling emergencies, our staff also serves the public and our responders by providing 
other administrative and non -emergency dispatch tasks. NVRDD staffs the communications center with multiple 
dispatchers and supervisors each shift to handle calls and requests. 
 
In 2022 NVRDD processed a total of 89,900 calls for service (CFS). Of these calls for service, 
32,067 were for the Town of Lunenburg. These calls for service average out to 2,673 CFS per 
month in the Town of and 88 CFS per day in the Town of Lunenburg. Each CFS represents one 
report entered into the Computer Aided Dispatch system by the dispatchers for the 9 -1-1 calls 
handled, the police, fire, or medical emergencies dispatched, and the other miscellaneous duties 
they perform on a daily basis. 
 
In 2022 NVRDD Telecommunicators answered 10,989 9-1-1 Calls. This total averages out to 916 
emergency 9- 1-1 calls per month and approximately 30 emergency 911 calls per day. NVRDD Staff prides itself in 
customer service and standards of care. Our average 9 -1-1 answer time is 3.5 seconds, which is approximately half of a 
normal telephone “ring”. Our staff is also evaluated monthly on their call handling skills and they rate at an average 94% 
adherence to the call taking protocols. 

===PAGE 91===
91 
NVRDD Telecommunicators average 32 hours of training per year. In 2022 NVRDD welcomed several new 
members to the team: three dispatchers, an administrative coordinator, a treasurer, and an accountant. NVRDD is 
approved for a total of 15 Full-Time Employees, and 3 Part-Time Employees. Currently NVRDD has 14 of the 15 
Full-Time Positions filled, and all 3 Part-Time Positions filled. NVRDD is projected to be fully staffed by the 
beginning of FY24. 
Conclusion: 
 
I would like to thank the Citizens of Lunenburg for their continued support. Without collaboration with our 
Community we would not be successful. We look forward to the New Year, and to continue to bring the very best in 
policing to our Community. 
I would also like to thank the women and men of the Lunenburg Police Department for their dedication to the 
Community, and the work that they perform on a daily basis. The personnel of the Department play a vital role in 
making Lunenburg a great place to live, work and visit for all. 
Thank you, 
 
Chief Thomas L. Gammel

CAPITAL PLANNING  REPORT 
 
Fiscal Year 2023 Capital Plan
 
 
The Capital Planning Committee began the capital planning process for Fiscal Year 2023 in September 2021.  The Capital Planni ng 
documents were sent to departments in late September and the Capital Planning Committee met with the departments throughout 
November and December to review all the capital project requests as a group.  The Capital Planning Committee prioritized 31 c apital 
projects. One project was removed due to not receiving a cost estimate and one project has been pushed out to FY24 as more 
information was needed. The remaining 29 projects totaled $3,861,049. The Committee presented the prioritized list to the Tow n 
Manager in January as required by the Town Charter and the Town Manager is recommending funding 23 of the prioritized project s 
totaling $2,162,849.

PLANNING BOARD 
Fourteen (14) ANR Plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law, but still 
requiring Board endorsement) were approved.   
 
Lunenburg’s Stormwater Bylaw requires Board review and approval for any development/redevelopment project that 
disturbs one acre or more of land.  This Bylaw safeguards the public health, safety, welfare and the environment by 
minimizing adverse impacts of contaminated stormwater both off-site and downstream.  These updates are more likely to 
affect large commercial projects and multi -family dwellings; not the average homeowner wanting to put in a shed, 
garden, pool, etc.  The Board approved stormwater permits for the following: 
 
 A warehouse off Leominster-Shirley Road; 
 A single-family house lot at 324 Flat Hill Road; 
 Construction of a 7-bay garage at 225 & 259 Leominster-Shirley Road; 
 The creation of four (4) lots at Cross and Mulpus; 
 The creation of six (6) lots on the corner of Holman and Northfield Road. 
 
Lunenburg has four designated Scenic Roads; Lancaster Avenue, Flat Hill Road, Northfield Road and Leominster Road.  
Over the past year you may have noticed building lots, such as some of those mentioned in the paragraph above, being 
created on some of these roads.  As these are Scenic Roads, any repair, maintenance, re -construction, or paving work in 
the Town’s right-of-way cannot involve the cutting or removal of trees or the tearing down or destruction of stone walls 
without consent of the Planning Board.   
 
The Commonwealth issued Site Eligibility for a proposed 40B development off Howard Street and the Board provided 
its comments to the Town Manager.  The Planning Board in conjunction with many other Boards, Commissions, Town 
Officials, and private citizens submitted numerous comments outlining the Town’s opposition to the development of 200 
apartment units at 400 Howard St. The development of affordable housing is critical to Lunenburg; however, the site in 
question has significant flaws that concern Town Officials.  The applicant is currently going through the public hearing 
process with the Zoning Board of Appeals. 
 
The Board submitted four articles for November 2021 Special Town Meeting. 
 Two of the articles, which passed, updated the zoning bylaw to reflect the current zoning; 
 Two articles were submitted on behalf of the Water District to update the Watershed Protection Map.  DEP 
requested additional changes to the proposed bylaw which Board members were not comfortable with; therefore, the 
Board chose to pass over the Water District articles.   
 
The Land Use Director worked on a new draft for 2022 Annual Town Meeting.  The two Water District articles were 
submitted for May 2022 town meeting, but did not pass.   
A third article was also submitted for 2022 Annual Town Meeting which added clarification to the permitting process for 
non-medical marijuana establishments.  That article passed. 
Uma Flowers, a cannabis retail facility, was awarded a Community Host Agreement from Lunenburg and submitted an 
application for a state -licensed retail establishment under Section 250 -4.15. Non-Medical Marijuana Establishments of 
the zoning bylaw.  The establishment will be located at 43 Summer Street (site of the former Donnelly’s Tavern).   
 
An application was received for construction of a 367,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building at 260  
Falulah Road.  The majority of the building was located on Airport Road/Crawford Street in Fitchburg, but a portion 
extended into Lunenburg.   
 
Approval was given for the construction of a 372,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building off Leominster -
Shirley Road near the Shirley town line.  As a result of this approval, improvements were made to Leominster -Shirley 
Road with the addition of an eastbound left -turn lane into the project and the widening of the road to the north (towards 
Reservoir Road) on the project side of the street. 
 
Under the Unitil Reliability Project, Unitil submitted an application for the removal and/or pruning of public shade trees 
along Leominster Road from Sunny Hill Road to Kilburn Street.  Some of the proposed trees already had contact with 
existing power lines.  Poles would be replaced and lines placed three to seven feet higher than the current placement.  By 
upgrading the existing lines Unitil expected to reduce outage time for incidents that occurred from the center of town to 
Goodrich Street.   
 
===PAGE 96===
96 
The Planning Board requested and was approved for Capital Plan Funding to begin an update of the 2002 Master Plan. 
This update will likely begin in FY23 and carry through FY25. The process will include public visioning sessions, input 
from as many stakeholders as possible, and will aid in moving Lunenburg and its policies through the next decade and 
beyond. 
 
Legislation passed requiring any MBTA community to have a zoning district of reasonable size in which multi -family 
housing would be permitted as of right.  A minimum density of 15 housing units per acre was required.  The town is 
currently working on compliance. The results and approach to complying with the requirements outlined in in MGL 
Chapter 40A Section 3A will be done through a public process by the Planning Board.  
 
The Planning Board created an Economic Development Committee which began meeting in April 2021. Over the past 
year the Committee has worked to build a relationship with the Lunenburg Business Association, the Commonwealth of 
Massachusetts Office of Business Development, and other stakeholders to provide policy recommendations to the 
Planning Board and create an environment that supports existing and proposed business in Lunenburg.  
 
Meetings resumed at Town Hall with a hybrid model; both in-person and via Zoom.   
 
Paula Bertram announced she would not seek another term at May 2022 town election.  Amanda Reid was elected to the 
Board to serve until 2027.   
 
The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be reached at 978 -
582-4147 extension 5.  Various forms are available online at https://lunenburgma.gov/ on the Planning Board ’s 
homepage.  The Zoning Map, Code of the Town of Lunenburg, MA (which contains the Protective [Zoning] Bylaws), 
Rules and Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land, Rules & Regulations for Site Plan Approval & Special 
Permits, and Permitting Guidebook are available online.   
 
 
 
 
Ad Hoc Open Space Advisory Committee to the Planning Board  
 
The Open Space Committee’s efforts in FY’22 principally focused on gaining a better understanding of the Town of 
Lunenburg’s portfolio of vacant, undeveloped, “general municipal” lands, and assessing which of those properties may 
be repurposed more specifically to meet needs identified in the Town’s Open Space and Recreation Plan. 
The Open Space Committee also reviewed several Chapter 61 withdrawal notifications this past year as part of the 
town’s defined multi-board process and reviewed the tax sale/taking process in the Spring of 2022. 
The Open Space Committee investigated two parcels on Lake Shirley held by the Town and reviewed them for possible 
lake access (car top kayak and canoe launch) for Lunenburg residents.

          ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS 
 
The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2022.   There were several applicants seeking 
special permits and special permits for dimensional variations.  The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and 
fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00PM on an “as needed” basis or ZOOM as an alternative. 
On August 18, 2022 a 40B Comprehensive Permit was filed for property located at 318, 319 & 400 Howard Street, for a 
project known as Pond View Commons owned by The Homes at Aro Estates, LLC.   
The proposal is to construct three multifamily residential buildings under the Massachusetts Comprehensive Permit 
Statute, Massachusetts General Law’s c.40B, ss.20-23.  
The applicant Pond View Commons, LLC seeks to create 200 apartment units within three multifamily residential 
buildings.   
The Zoning Board consists of the following members:  Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice -Chairman, 
Members:  Hans Wentrup, David Blatt, Patrick Callahan and Anthony Nicastro and Donald Gurney, Associate members.   
Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board. 
The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows: 
1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town’s Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws. 
2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town’s Protective By-laws. 
3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as 
amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from 
the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other 
Administrative Official. 
4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 40B.  
The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under Chapter 
40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.  These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning 
ordinances. 
The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue, 
Lunenburg.  Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and 
Thursday, closed Friday.  I 
f you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978 -582-4146 X 421 during regular 
business hours.  Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at www.Lunenburgma.gov.

Lunenburg Public Schools 
Superintendent’s Message 
I am pleased to present my annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools.  I want to express my gratitude for a 
collaborative approach toward projecting and planning for current and future needs with the Town Manager, Heather 
Lemieux.  As the leader of the district, I am fortunate to work with a School Committee that is truly dedicated to serving 
the best interests of our students and appreciative that the Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee work 
collaboratively with the School Committee and School Department to problem-solve in order to better meet the 
educational needs of the children of Lunenburg. 
 
The district has remained focused on our Strategy for Continuous Improvement, which is aligned with our Mission and 
Vision: 
The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for life-
long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, and rewarding lives. 
 
The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of attending to 
the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered instruction for deeper 
learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills necessary for continuous learning for all. 
 
The Strategy is a living document.  Each August the District Leadership Team reviews the Strategy, its objectives and 
action plans. The Strategy objectives for the next two to three years are: 
 
Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student -centered and inclusive 
by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential work for transforming 
practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually learning and adapting practice to 
meet the needs of all students.  
Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous learning in 
a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values.  
 
Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication, in order to 
increase the organization’s capacity to execute the district’s mission and vision. 
 
Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are provided equal 
access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with an appropriate level of 
support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient, confident, and capable young adults 
adequately prepared for future challenges.  
 
Over the last several years we have worked to put in place the necessary infrastructure to support the academic 
achievement of all students.  We have established curriculum leadership positions, instructional specialists/coaches, 
teacher curriculum leaders; piloted and purchased evidence-based math and literacy curricula for Kindergarten (K) 
through grade 5; implemented a standards-based benchmark assessment in math and literacy for grades K through 8; 
provided job-embedded professional development for teachers in best instructional practices and using assessment data 
to inform their instruction; and hired tutoring/intervention staff for additional academic supports during the school day as 
well as after school in order to close learning gaps. The Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator 
positions have been and continue to be integral to all of this work. 
 
In order for students to be ready and able to learn in the classroom, behavioral health needs must be addressed.  To that 
end, we have developed a multi-tiered system of added guidance counselors, social workers and adjustment counselors; 
piloted and adopted a districtwide social-emotional learning curriculum.  Students receive social-emotional supports in 
small group with counselors or in a one-on-one setting when student need dictates.  Additionally, we have continued to 
provide Care Solace, an online resource with live 24/7 concierge meant to assist families and individuals in finding local 
mental health support. Care Solace is a tool for school staff and families to connect with community-based mental 
healthcare resources and providers. The vast database of mental health resources includes vetted local therapists and 
programs.  
 
All of these supports have been absolutely critical in order to respond to the increase in significant behavioral health 
needs of our students.  
 
 
===PAGE 101===
101 
Superintendent’s Message 
The district has been working with intent to foster a safe and caring environment and inclusive culture in our schools and 
district.  Last year we administered the National School Climate Center survey for families, students, and staff.  We 
analyzed results and used that data to develop goals to improve our schools’ climate.  We will repeat that survey this 
spring to see if changes have had any impact.  As we look to build a more inclusive culture in our schools, we also look 
to diversify our staff to be more reflective of our community.  To that end, a district team participated in the Department 
of Elementary and Secondary Education’s Teacher Diversification Professional Learning Network and as a result, have 
been examining our recruitment and hiring practices.  The Lunenburg School Committee is also committed to this work 
and requested an equity audit for our district.  The audit commenced in July 2022 and the final report was made available 
to the Lunenburg community in February 2023 via the district website with a request for feedback.  The District 
Leadership will use this report to develop action plans which will be included in the district’s Strategy for Continuous 
Improvement.  Once these plans are developed, as is our annual practice, the administration will seek the Lunenburg 
School Committee’s endorsement.  We look forward to engaging in this work with stakeholders.  
 
Another way the district is seeking to build a more inclusive culture is through strengthening the family-school 
partnership.  This year Luneburg Public Schools was one of four districts/schools selected to participate in the 
Department of Elementary and Secondary Education’s Family-School Partnership Initiative Program.  We will be 
expanding this work to involve stakeholders. This work will also result in the development of action plans, which will be 
incorporated into the Strategy for Continuous Improvement.    
 
It is important to note that the physical environment of our schools is also important to the learning experience of our 
students.  Turkey Hill Elementary School is an aging facility which was not designed for elementary age students.  It was 
originally a middle school.  The building presents challenges and limitations for our teachers in terms of delivery of 
instruction in the modern era.  Electrical outlets are extremely limited in the classrooms and the lack of energy efficient 
windows creates a challenging learning environment with very high and very low outdoor temperatures.  The building 
has aging systems that cannot be easily, quickly, or inexpensively addressed.  With each passing year, the risk of system 
failures looms larger.  It is important for the school district administration, School Committee, and other boards and 
committees to continue to engage in discussion regarding the future of Turkey Hill Elementary School.   
 
All of these individual efforts and discussions regarding the future are designed to have a cumulative impact on student 
achievement and growth, and overall well-being in the post-COVID years and beyond.  I wish to acknowledge and thank 
our staff for their professionalism and flexibility as we have continued to make adjustments in order to provide the best 
possible learning environment and experience for our students; our parents and families for their continued support and 
partnership; and the Lunenburg School Committee, Town Manager, and local boards and committees for their support of 
the schools.  We remain focused on improving the ways in which we support our students academically, socially and 
emotionally in partnership with parents, extended families, and the greater community.  Through this work we are on a 
journey together. Collectively we have been and will continue to make a difference for our students.  I think Maya 
Angelou has captured our journey well when she said, “I have great respect for the past.  If you don’t know where 
you’ve come from, you don’t know where you’re going.  I have respect for the past, but I’m a person of the moment.  
I’m here, and I do my best to be completely centered at the place I’m at, then I go forward to the next place.”  
 
Annually I conclude my report with the announcement of the Superintendent’s Award recipient.  The selection process is 
always difficult as we have many hard-working, high-performing, community service-oriented students.  I am pleased to 
announce Cassandra Cohen as the recipient of the 2023 Superintendent’s Award.  Cassie’s academic accomplishments 
include: Academic Excellence Award in Foreign Language, English, History, Mathematics, and Science.  She is also a 
Smith Book Award recipient.  Cassie has been a member of the Science Olympiad, Golf, and Indoor Track teams.  She is 
a member of the World Hunger Task Force and has served as the Class Treasurer.  She has also volunteered for the 
Lunenburg Food Pantry, served as a peer tutor, and works as an intern in our district’s curriculum office.  Since the 8th 
grade, Cassie has been an assistant teacher at the Joseph C. Foster Religious School.  When asked for a quote that best 
describes her, she offered this Dolly Parton quote: “Find out who you are and do it on purpose.” 
 
Respectfully Submitted, 
 
Kate Burnham, Ed.D. 
Superintendent

ENVIRONMENTAL MONITORING  
During our annual mechanical rooms inspections conducted through our insurance company. It was determined the three 
(3) school building’s mechanical rooms were lacking carbon monoxide detection. Those will be remedied FY-2023. 
 
CAPITAL PROGRAM 
A new replacement 1 Ton Dump truck with a plow was approved and has been ordered. A new replacement campus 
commercial grade lawn mower was approved and has been ordered.

The department continued to focus on maintaining a high level of care on the three grass athletic fields at the LMHS

students.

Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology Department 
 
The Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology (IT) Department maintained a critical role in the expansion of 
the technology in our classrooms.  The Department worked with staff and students to redefine what classroom 
instruction looks and feels like for our students. 
 
The school year began with all students reporting back in session after the COVID restraints with each student having 
access to a device for daily instruction.  Here is a summary of the district device model: 
 
 Lunenburg Primary School – the building have enough iPads to assist with instruction and testing at the K-2 level 
 Turkey Hill Elementary School – each classroom is equipped with Chromebook carts 
 Lunenburg Middle High School – each student has access to a personal Chromebook, which they can bring home for 
their individual studies 
 
In addition, Turkey Hill Elementary School purchased a Touchview interactive screen for every classroom.   This 
equipment assists with the collaboration between teachers and students and has improved student participation.   
 
Reliance on computers in the classroom and in the households was prevalent when students were burdening the network 
system.  With the assistance from E-rate funding, the district purchased new equipment for the building server rooms, 
including routers, switches, wireless access points and licenses.  
 
Besides expanding educational needs, the IT Department maintained the typical response to maintain the district’s core 
technology services.  Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and explore 
innovations in education technology.  The district is still working with a consultant to audit current practices, 
applications and models to measure any areas for growth, improvement and capacity. 
 
Respectfully Submitted, 
 
Michael Cassidy 
Business Manager/HR Director
